## Read this before the notation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000025","sourceNodeId":"n0000025","sourceUnitIds":["u000024"],"sourceAnchor":"The guide introduces notation after an initial orientation.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The guide introduces notation after an initial orientation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000026","sourceNodeId":"n0000026","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets is a proposed graph-rewriting model.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ-Nets is a proposed graph-rewriting model.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000027","sourceNodeId":"n0000027","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"To use the model, convert a program into a graph of small nodes and wires, then repeatedly replace small matching graph pieces.","status":"source_ready"} -->

To use the model, convert a program into a graph of small nodes and wires, then repeatedly replace small matching graph pieces.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000568","sourceNodeId":"n0000568","sourceUnitIds":["u000024"],"sourceAnchor":"## Read this before the notation","status":"source_ready"} -->

## Read this before the notation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000569","sourceNodeId":"n0000569","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets is a proposed graph-rewriting model. Instead of walking a syntax tree, you turn a program into a graph of tiny nodes and wires, then repeatedly replace small matching pieces of that graph. The aim is optimal parallel reduction: do not spend effort on work that will later be thrown away, and do each necessary reduction once even when an expression is used in several places.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ-Nets is a proposed graph-rewriting model. Instead of walking a syntax tree, you turn a program into a graph of tiny nodes and wires, then repeatedly replace small matching pieces of that graph. The aim is optimal parallel reduction: do not spend effort on work that will later be thrown away, and do each necessary reduction once even when an expression is used in several places.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000659","sourceNodeId":"n0000659","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"The model avoids work that will later be discarded.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The model avoids work that will later be discarded.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d5f4b1e7e5e7b53f","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"expression","status":"expanded"} -->

An expression is a combination of values, names, operators, or function calls that can be evaluated to produce a result.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000660","sourceNodeId":"n0000660","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"The model performs necessary work only once even when an expression is used multiple times.","status":"expanded"} -->

The model performs the necessary work for an expression only once.

This remains true even when the same expression is used multiple times.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000028","sourceNodeId":"n0000028","sourceUnitIds":["u000025"],"sourceAnchor":"The model aims for optimal parallel reduction by avoiding work that will later be discarded and reducing necessary work once even when an expression has multiple uses.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The model aims for optimal parallel reduction by avoiding work that will later be discarded and reducing necessary work once even when an expression has multiple uses.

## The core idea

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000029","sourceNodeId":"n0000029","sourceUnitIds":["u000027"],"sourceAnchor":"A program is built as a net of very small agents.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A program is built as a net of very small agents.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000030","sourceNodeId":"n0000030","sourceUnitIds":["u000027"],"sourceAnchor":"Local pairs of agents can interact independently.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Local pairs of agents can interact independently.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000031","sourceNodeId":"n0000031","sourceUnitIds":["u000027"],"sourceAnchor":"Sharing is represented directly in the graph rather than in a separate copy table.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Sharing is represented directly in the graph rather than in a separate copy table.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000114","sourceNodeId":"n0000114","sourceUnitIds":["u000026"],"sourceAnchor":"### The core idea","status":"source_ready"} -->

### The core idea

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000570","sourceNodeId":"n0000570","sourceUnitIds":["u000027"],"sourceAnchor":"Build the program as a net of very small agents. Let local pairs interact on their own. Put sharing in the graph itself, not in a side table of copies.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Build the program as a net of very small agents. Let local pairs interact on their own. Put sharing in the graph itself, not in a side table of copies.

### Six ideas you need before Chapter 1

## λ-term

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000035","sourceNodeId":"n0000035","sourceUnitIds":["u000032"],"sourceAnchor":"A lambda term is a small functional program.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A lambda term is a small functional program.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000036","sourceNodeId":"n0000036","sourceUnitIds":["u000032"],"sourceAnchor":"The expression λx.t denotes a function with parameter x and body t.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The expression λx.t denotes a function with parameter x and body t.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000037","sourceNodeId":"n0000037","sourceUnitIds":["u000032"],"sourceAnchor":"The expression t u denotes applying t to u.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The expression t u denotes applying t to u.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000116","sourceNodeId":"n0000116","sourceUnitIds":["u000031"],"sourceAnchor":"#### λ-term","status":"source_ready"} -->

#### λ-term

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000438","sourceNodeId":"n0000438","sourceUnitIds":["u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"### λ-term","status":"source_ready"} -->

### λ-term

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000573","sourceNodeId":"n0000573","sourceUnitIds":["u000032"],"sourceAnchor":"A tiny functional program. λx.t is a function with parameter x and body t. t u means “apply t to u.”","status":"source_ready"} -->

A tiny functional program. λx.t is a function with parameter x and body t. t u means “apply t to u.”

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000439","sourceNodeId":"n0000439","sourceUnitIds":["u000398"],"sourceAnchor":"A λ-term is a program made from variables, abstractions, and applications.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A λ-term is a program made from variables, abstractions, and applications.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000440","sourceNodeId":"n0000440","sourceUnitIds":["u000398"],"sourceAnchor":"In λx.t, the abstraction binds x in body t.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In λx.t, the abstraction binds x in body t.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000441","sourceNodeId":"n0000441","sourceUnitIds":["u000398"],"sourceAnchor":"In t u, t is the function and u is its argument.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In t u, t is the function and u is its argument.

## Bound vs. free

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000038","sourceNodeId":"n0000038","sourceUnitIds":["u000034"],"sourceAnchor":"An occurrence of x is bound when a surrounding λx owns it.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An occurrence of x is bound when a surrounding λx owns it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000039","sourceNodeId":"n0000039","sourceUnitIds":["u000034"],"sourceAnchor":"An occurrence is free when no surrounding abstraction owns it.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An occurrence is free when no surrounding abstraction owns it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000117","sourceNodeId":"n0000117","sourceUnitIds":["u000033"],"sourceAnchor":"#### Bound vs. free","status":"source_ready"} -->

#### Bound vs. free

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000575","sourceNodeId":"n0000575","sourceUnitIds":["u000034"],"sourceAnchor":"An occurrence of x is bound when a surrounding λx owns it. It is free when no surrounding abstraction does—like a name that still comes from outside the term.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An occurrence of x is bound when a surrounding λx owns it. It is free when no surrounding abstraction does—like a name that still comes from outside the term.

## Levels & Δ

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000040","sourceNodeId":"n0000040","sourceUnitIds":["u000036"],"sourceAnchor":"Levels track nested abstractions.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Levels track nested abstractions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000041","sourceNodeId":"n0000041","sourceUnitIds":["u000036"],"sourceAnchor":"A level shift adds a delta cost when a variable crosses a lambda.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A level shift adds a delta cost when a variable crosses a lambda.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000042","sourceNodeId":"n0000042","sourceUnitIds":["u000036"],"sourceAnchor":"Levels and deltas track scopes without global lookups.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Levels and deltas track scopes without global lookups.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000043","sourceNodeId":"n0000043","sourceUnitIds":["u000037"],"sourceAnchor":"The first chapter introduces the motivation.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The first chapter introduces the motivation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000118","sourceNodeId":"n0000118","sourceUnitIds":["u000035"],"sourceAnchor":"#### Levels & Δ","status":"source_ready"} -->

#### Levels & Δ

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000576","sourceNodeId":"n0000576","sourceUnitIds":["u000036"],"sourceAnchor":"Levels track nested abstractions. Each level shift adds a cost (a \"delta\") when a variable travels through a lambda. This is how we keep track of scopes without global lookups.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Levels track nested abstractions. Each level shift adds a cost (a "delta") when a variable travels through a lambda. This is how we keep track of scopes without global lookups.

01 Motivation

## The problem is wasted work.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000044","sourceNodeId":"n0000044","sourceUnitIds":["u000038"],"sourceAnchor":"The central problem is wasted work.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The central problem is wasted work.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000045","sourceNodeId":"n0000045","sourceUnitIds":["u000039"],"sourceAnchor":"Ordinary lambda calculus is compact but leaves open what to do when a variable is unused or appears multiple times.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Ordinary lambda calculus is compact but leaves open what to do when a variable is unused or appears multiple times.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000046","sourceNodeId":"n0000046","sourceUnitIds":["u000040"],"sourceAnchor":"The mechanics section introduces connections.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The mechanics section introduces connections.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000578","sourceNodeId":"n0000578","sourceUnitIds":["u000038"],"sourceAnchor":"## The problem is wasted work.","status":"source_ready"} -->

## The problem is wasted work.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000579","sourceNodeId":"n0000579","sourceUnitIds":["u000039"],"sourceAnchor":"Ordinary λ-calculus is wonderfully compact. The familiar story—“replace a variable with an argument”—hides a cost question: what should happen when the variable is unused, or when it appears many times?","status":"source_ready"} -->

Ordinary λ-calculus is wonderfully compact. The familiar story—“replace a variable with an argument”—hides a cost question: what should happen when the variable is unused, or when it appears many times?

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000580","sourceNodeId":"n0000580","sourceUnitIds":["u000040"],"sourceAnchor":"03 Mechanics","status":"source_ready"} -->

03 Mechanics

## The anatomy of a connection

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000047","sourceNodeId":"n0000047","sourceUnitIds":["u000042"],"sourceAnchor":"Interaction nets model lambda terms as wiring.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Interaction nets model lambda terms as wiring.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000048","sourceNodeId":"n0000048","sourceUnitIds":["u000042"],"sourceAnchor":"A lambda node has a binding port for the parameter and a body port for the expression.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A lambda node has a binding port for the parameter and a body port for the expression.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000049","sourceNodeId":"n0000049","sourceUnitIds":["u000042"],"sourceAnchor":"Variable distribution determines the logic of the wiring.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Variable distribution determines the logic of the wiring.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000050","sourceNodeId":"n0000050","sourceUnitIds":["u000043"],"sourceAnchor":"The delta symbol denotes Δ.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The delta symbol denotes Δ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000119","sourceNodeId":"n0000119","sourceUnitIds":["u000041"],"sourceAnchor":"## The anatomy of a connection","status":"source_ready"} -->

## The anatomy of a connection

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000581","sourceNodeId":"n0000581","sourceUnitIds":["u000042"],"sourceAnchor":"In interaction nets, we model λ-terms as wiring. The λ-node has a binding port (for the parameter) and a body port (for the expression). The logic depends on how variables are distributed.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In interaction nets, we model λ-terms as wiring. The λ-node has a binding port (for the parameter) and a body port (for the expression). The logic depends on how variables are distributed.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000582","sourceNodeId":"n0000582","sourceUnitIds":["u000043"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ

## The "Why" of Deltas

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000051","sourceNodeId":"n0000051","sourceUnitIds":["u000045"],"sourceAnchor":"A delta represents the cost of crossing a scope.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A delta represents the cost of crossing a scope.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000052","sourceNodeId":"n0000052","sourceUnitIds":["u000045"],"sourceAnchor":"When a variable moves from one lambda into another, its depth increases.","status":"source_ready"} -->

When a variable moves from one lambda into another, its depth increases.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000053","sourceNodeId":"n0000053","sourceUnitIds":["u000045"],"sourceAnchor":"Deltas let the interaction net locate variables without scanning the code.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Deltas let the interaction net locate variables without scanning the code.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000120","sourceNodeId":"n0000120","sourceUnitIds":["u000044"],"sourceAnchor":"### The \"Why\" of Deltas","status":"source_ready"} -->

### The "Why" of Deltas

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000583","sourceNodeId":"n0000583","sourceUnitIds":["u000045"],"sourceAnchor":"Deltas are not just numbers; they are the cost of crossing a scope. When a variable moves out of one lambda and into another, it gains depth. Deltas ensure that the interaction net \"knows\" where its variables live without ever scanning the code.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Deltas are not just numbers; they are the cost of crossing a scope. When a variable moves out of one lambda and into another, it gains depth. Deltas ensure that the interaction net "knows" where its variables live without ever scanning the code.

## How connections resolve

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000054","sourceNodeId":"n0000054","sourceUnitIds":["u000047"],"sourceAnchor":"When an application node meets a lambda abstraction, the two nodes interact.","status":"source_ready"} -->

When an application node meets a lambda abstraction, the two nodes interact.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000055","sourceNodeId":"n0000055","sourceUnitIds":["u000047"],"sourceAnchor":"The lambda node’s binding port determines whether to replicate the variable or erase it.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The lambda node’s binding port determines whether to replicate the variable or erase it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000121","sourceNodeId":"n0000121","sourceUnitIds":["u000046"],"sourceAnchor":"### How connections resolve","status":"source_ready"} -->

### How connections resolve

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000584","sourceNodeId":"n0000584","sourceUnitIds":["u000047"],"sourceAnchor":"When an application node (the @) meets a λ-abstraction, they interact. The λ-node's binding port determines if it needs to replicate the variable (using a Replicator node) or erase it (if it doesn't appear in the body).","status":"source_ready"} -->

When an application node (the @) meets a λ-abstraction, they interact. The λ-node's binding port determines if it needs to replicate the variable (using a Replicator node) or erase it (if it doesn't appear in the body).

A redex is a place you can simplify right now—here, a function already sitting next to its argument. A reduction is one such step.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000588","sourceNodeId":"n0000588","sourceUnitIds":["u000052"],"sourceAnchor":"The substitution view","status":"source_ready"} -->

The substitution view

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000589","sourceNodeId":"n0000589","sourceUnitIds":["u000054"],"sourceAnchor":"That one sentence hides two very different edge cases. They are where the paper begins.","status":"source_ready"} -->

That one sentence hides two very different edge cases. They are where the paper begins.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000590","sourceNodeId":"n0000590","sourceUnitIds":["u000055"],"sourceAnchor":"Erasure","status":"source_ready"} -->

Erasure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d1bc7dcb638a350b","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"lambda abstraction","status":"expanded"} -->

A lambda abstraction is an expression of the form λx.M that defines a function, where x is the input variable and M is the function body. Within M, occurrences of x refer to the input supplied to that function.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-61a48d42f2277a2c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"substitution","status":"expanded"} -->

Substitution replaces the free occurrences of a target variable in one expression with another expression. An occurrence is free when it is not inside a lambda abstraction that binds that same target variable.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-84bd07f7ad5fb152","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"free occurrence","status":"expanded"} -->

A free occurrence is an appearance of a variable that is not controlled by an enclosing lambda abstraction with the same variable name. In λx.(x y), x is controlled by λx, while y has a free occurrence.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6f60789809a0d7ca","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"variable capture","status":"expanded"} -->

Variable capture occurs when substitution makes a variable that was free in the inserted expression become controlled by an enclosing lambda abstraction. Capture-avoiding substitution renames a conflicting bound variable before performing the replacement.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001027","sourceNodeId":"n0001027","sourceUnitIds":["u000053"],"sourceAnchor":"Applying a lambda abstraction to an argument replaces x in t with u: (λx. t) u → t[x := u].","status":"expanded"} -->

Applying a lambda abstraction λx. t to an argument u forms the expression (λx. t) u.

**Derivation.** The application reduces according to (λx. t) u → t[x := u].

The substitution t[x := u] replaces free occurrences of x in t with u while avoiding capture of variables in u by surrounding binders.

## Work that gets discarded

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000065","sourceNodeId":"n0000065","sourceUnitIds":["u000057"],"sourceAnchor":"If the body t never mentions x, the argument u disappears.","status":"source_ready"} -->

If the body t never mentions x, the argument u disappears.

Work performed inside an argument that is later erased is wasted.

### Work that gets duplicated

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000593","sourceNodeId":"n0000593","sourceUnitIds":["u000060"],"sourceAnchor":"If x occurs more than once, naive substitution copies u. If u still contains redexes, you may redo the same necessary work in every copy.","status":"source_ready"} -->

If x occurs more than once, naive substitution copies u. If u still contains redexes, you may redo the same necessary work in every copy.

## 1.2 The four familiar fragments of the λ-calculus

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000070","sourceNodeId":"n0000070","sourceUnitIds":["u000061"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper organizes lambda calculus fragments by variable-use rules.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The paper organizes lambda calculus fragments by variable-use rules.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000071","sourceNodeId":"n0000071","sourceUnitIds":["u000062"],"sourceAnchor":"A bound variable is an occurrence owned by a surrounding lambda.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A bound variable is an occurrence owned by a surrounding lambda.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000072","sourceNodeId":"n0000072","sourceUnitIds":["u000062"],"sourceAnchor":"In λx.x, the occurrence of x is bound by the surrounding lambda.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In λx.x, the occurrence of x is bound by the surrounding lambda.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000073","sourceNodeId":"n0000073","sourceUnitIds":["u000062"],"sourceAnchor":"The linear calculus requires every bound variable to occur exactly once.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The linear calculus requires every bound variable to occur exactly once.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000594","sourceNodeId":"n0000594","sourceUnitIds":["u000061"],"sourceAnchor":"### 1.2 The four familiar fragments of the λ-calculus","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 1.2 The four familiar fragments of the λ-calculus

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000595","sourceNodeId":"n0000595","sourceUnitIds":["u000062"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper frames the issue with a simple grid of variable-use rules. A bound variable is an occurrence owned by a surrounding λ—the x in λx.x, for example. Start from the linear calculus, where every bound variable appears exactly once. Then allow omission, repetition, or both.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The paper frames the issue with a simple grid of variable-use rules. A bound variable is an occurrence owned by a surrounding λ—the x in λx.x, for example.

Start from the linear calculus, where every bound variable appears exactly once. Then allow omission, repetition, or both.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000074","sourceNodeId":"n0000074","sourceUnitIds":["u000064"],"sourceAnchor":"λL is the linear fragment in which each bound variable occurs exactly once.","status":"source_ready"} -->

λL is the linear fragment in which each bound variable occurs exactly once.

A reduction order chooses which redex to simplify next.

### 1.3 Why choosing a clever order is not enough

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000605","sourceNodeId":"n0000605","sourceUnitIds":["u000075"],"sourceAnchor":"A reduction order says which redex to simplify next. For erasure, normal order—outermost application before its argument—can avoid reducing something a function is about to throw away. Sharing is harder. The paper follows Lévy: some shared terms have no ordinary sequential order that avoids every duplicated reduction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A reduction order says which redex to simplify next. For erasure, normal order—outermost application before its argument—can avoid reducing something a function is about to throw away.

Sharing is harder. The paper follows Lévy: some shared terms have no ordinary sequential order that avoids every duplicated reduction.

Why a net at all? A graph can keep one identity for many uses. Δ-Nets is one local way to manage that identity while the graph rewrites.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001030","sourceNodeId":"n0001030","sourceUnitIds":["u000087"],"sourceAnchor":"Two uses can share one intermediate share component.","status":"expanded"} -->

A share component is an intermediate component through which a value or resource can be used.

Two uses can connect to one intermediate share component, so they share that component rather than using separate intermediate components.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001031","sourceNodeId":"n0001031","sourceUnitIds":["u000087"],"sourceAnchor":"The shared component can feed one argument.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The shared component can feed one argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d39d6e6c1ee43e23","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"parameter port","status":"expanded"} -->

A parameter port is a named input or connection point through which a component receives a parameter value from another part of a system.

## 2.2 Interaction systems, in one minute

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000103","sourceNodeId":"n0000103","sourceUnitIds":["u000089"],"sourceAnchor":"The guide gives a short introduction to interaction systems.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The guide gives a short introduction to interaction systems.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000104","sourceNodeId":"n0000104","sourceUnitIds":["u000090"],"sourceAnchor":"An interaction system consists of agents and interaction rules.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An interaction system consists of agents and interaction rules.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000105","sourceNodeId":"n0000105","sourceUnitIds":["u000090"],"sourceAnchor":"An agent is a small node with sockets called ports.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An agent is a small node with sockets called ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000106","sourceNodeId":"n0000106","sourceUnitIds":["u000090"],"sourceAnchor":"The principal port is the distinguished port that can initiate a rewrite.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The principal port is the distinguished port that can initiate a rewrite.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000107","sourceNodeId":"n0000107","sourceUnitIds":["u000090"],"sourceAnchor":"Auxiliary ports carry surrounding wiring through a rewrite.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Auxiliary ports carry surrounding wiring through a rewrite.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000108","sourceNodeId":"n0000108","sourceUnitIds":["u000091"],"sourceAnchor":"Two agents meeting principal-to-principal form an active pair.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Two agents meeting principal-to-principal form an active pair.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000109","sourceNodeId":"n0000109","sourceUnitIds":["u000091"],"sourceAnchor":"An interaction rule rewrites an active pair by reconnecting only the wires attached at that location.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An interaction rule rewrites an active pair by reconnecting only the wires attached at that location.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000110","sourceNodeId":"n0000110","sourceUnitIds":["u000091"],"sourceAnchor":"The active pair and its nearby wires are the complete input to the rule.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The active pair and its nearby wires are the complete input to the rule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000111","sourceNodeId":"n0000111","sourceUnitIds":["u000092","u000093"],"sourceAnchor":"The first step is to find an active pair.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The first step is to find an active pair.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000617","sourceNodeId":"n0000617","sourceUnitIds":["u000089"],"sourceAnchor":"### 2.2 Interaction systems, in one minute","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 2.2 Interaction systems, in one minute

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000618","sourceNodeId":"n0000618","sourceUnitIds":["u000090"],"sourceAnchor":"An interaction system has agents and interaction rules. An agent is a small node with sockets called ports. One socket is special—the principal port—and that is what can start a rewrite. The rest are auxiliary ports; they carry the surrounding wiring through the rewrite.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An interaction system has agents and interaction rules. An agent is a small node with sockets called ports.

One socket is special—the principal port—and that is what can start a rewrite. The rest are auxiliary ports; they carry the surrounding wiring through the rewrite.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000619","sourceNodeId":"n0000619","sourceUnitIds":["u000091"],"sourceAnchor":"When two agents meet principal-to-principal, they form an active pair. A rule rewrites that pattern by reconnecting only the wires attached right there. The pair and those nearby wires are the rule’s entire input.","status":"source_ready"} -->

When two agents meet principal-to-principal, they form an active pair. A rule rewrites that pattern by reconnecting only the wires attached right there. The pair and those nearby wires are the rule’s entire input.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000620","sourceNodeId":"n0000620","sourceUnitIds":["u000092"],"sourceAnchor":"01","status":"source_ready"} -->

01

## Find an active pair

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000112","sourceNodeId":"n0000112","sourceUnitIds":["u000094"],"sourceAnchor":"Only a principal-to-principal wire counts as an active pair.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Only a principal-to-principal wire counts as an active pair.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000113","sourceNodeId":"n0000113","sourceUnitIds":["u000095","u000096"],"sourceAnchor":"The second step is to apply the active pair’s local rule.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The second step is to apply the active pair’s local rule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000621","sourceNodeId":"n0000621","sourceUnitIds":["u000093"],"sourceAnchor":"### Find an active pair","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Find an active pair

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000622","sourceNodeId":"n0000622","sourceUnitIds":["u000094"],"sourceAnchor":"Only a principal-to-principal wire counts. Each local decision stays sharply bounded.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Only a principal-to-principal wire counts. Each local decision stays sharply bounded.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000624","sourceNodeId":"n0000624","sourceUnitIds":["u000095","u000096"],"sourceAnchor":"Step 2 applies an active pair's local rule.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Step 2 applies an active pair's local rule.

## Apply its local rule

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000135","sourceNodeId":"n0000135","sourceUnitIds":["u000097"],"sourceAnchor":"The pair either disappears or passes through the other, reconnecting only nearby wires. That single application is one interaction step.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The pair either disappears or passes through the other, reconnecting only nearby wires. That single application is one interaction step.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000136","sourceNodeId":"n0000136","sourceUnitIds":["u000098"],"sourceAnchor":"03","status":"source_ready"} -->

03

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001033","sourceNodeId":"n0001033","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Distinct active pairs never share an agent because each agent has one principal port.","status":"expanded"} -->

Each agent has one principal port, which is the port used to identify its principal interaction connection.

**Derivation.** Distinct active pairs never share an agent because an agent's single principal port cannot participate in two distinct active pairs.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001035","sourceNodeId":"n0001035","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Distinct active-pair rewrites commute because they use distinct agents and leave the same nearby wiring in either order.","status":"expanded"} -->

Distinct active-pair rewrites commute, so either rewrite may be performed first.

**Derivation.** They commute because they use distinct agents and leave the same nearby wiring regardless of which rewrite is performed first.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001036","sourceNodeId":"n0001036","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Confluence and termination are separate: some nets reduce forever.","status":"expanded"} -->

Confluence and termination are distinct properties of a reduction system such as a net.

Some nets are non-terminating, meaning that they can continue reducing forever.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001037","sourceNodeId":"n0001037","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"When a core interaction order normalizes, every normalizing order reaches the same result in the same number of interactions.","status":"expanded"} -->

A core interaction order is a strategy for deciding which local net interaction to perform next.

When a core interaction order normalizes, every normalizing order reaches the same result after the same number of interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001038","sourceNodeId":"n0001038","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Earlier optimal-reduction nets used indexed fans and delimiter agents to track sharing scopes.","status":"expanded"} -->

Earlier optimal-reduction nets used indexed fans to track sharing scopes.

Those nets also used delimiter agents to track the same sharing-scope information.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001039","sourceNodeId":"n0001039","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets replaces separate sharing-scope bookkeeping with a variable-arity replicator carrying a level and per-port deltas.","status":"expanded"} -->

Δ-Nets replaces separate bookkeeping for sharing scopes.

Instead, a variable-arity replicator carries a level and a delta value for each port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001040","sourceNodeId":"n0001040","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"A Δ-Net core contains fans, erasers, and replicators.","status":"expanded"} -->

Fans are among the agent kinds contained in a Δ-Net core.

Erasers and replicators are also among the agent kinds contained in that core.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001041","sourceNodeId":"n0001041","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"A fan has exactly two auxiliary ports and represents lambda-term application and abstraction structure.","status":"expanded"} -->

A fan has exactly two auxiliary ports.

Within the net, a fan represents application and abstraction structure from a lambda term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001042","sourceNodeId":"n0001042","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"An eraser has no auxiliary ports and marks an unused bound variable.","status":"expanded"} -->

An eraser has no auxiliary ports.

An eraser marks a bound variable that is unused.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001043","sourceNodeId":"n0001043","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator has any natural-number auxiliary arity and joins uses of one shared value.","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator can have any natural-number auxiliary arity.

It joins uses of one shared value.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001044","sourceNodeId":"n0001044","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator level names its sharing context, while each auxiliary port delta adjusts a replica's level when it exits that port.","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator level identifies the sharing context in which that replicator operates.

When a replica leaves through an auxiliary port, the port’s delta changes the replica’s level according to that port’s adjustment.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001045","sourceNodeId":"n0001045","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔL permits fans only; ΔA adds erasers; ΔI adds replicators; ΔK permits all three agents.","status":"expanded"} -->

ΔL, ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK are four variants with different permitted agent sets.

ΔL permits fans only; ΔA adds erasers; ΔI adds replicators; and ΔK permits all three agents: fans, erasers, and replicators.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001046","sourceNodeId":"n0001046","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Linear terms have exactly one bound use, affine terms at most one, relevant terms at least one, and full terms any count.","status":"expanded"} -->

A linear term has exactly one bound use.

An affine term has at most one bound use.

A relevant term has at least one bound use.

A full term permits any number of bound uses.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001048","sourceNodeId":"n0001048","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"For well-formed nets translated from lambda terms, active replicators at the same level are already equal.","status":"expanded"} -->

In a well-formed net obtained by translating a lambda term, any two active replicators located at the same level are equal.

Thus, when reduction compares active replicators at one level, it does not need to handle unequal replicator shapes: well-formedness guarantees that they already match.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001049","sourceNodeId":"n0001049","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Annihilation removes equal agents and reconnects corresponding auxiliary ports.","status":"expanded"} -->

Annihilation is a reduction step that removes two equal agents from the net.

After removing the equal agents, annihilation reconnects each auxiliary port of one agent to the corresponding auxiliary port of the other agent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001050","sourceNodeId":"n0001050","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Erasure deletes the distinct agent met by an eraser because the eraser has no auxiliary ports.","status":"expanded"} -->

An eraser removes the distinct agent that it encounters.

This deletion occurs because the eraser has no auxiliary ports through which it could preserve or redirect the agent's additional connections.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001051","sourceNodeId":"n0001051","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Distinct non-eraser agents commute, producing outgoing copies according to the other agent's auxiliary-port count.","status":"expanded"} -->

Two distinct agents that are not erasers commute, meaning their order of interaction does not change the resulting structure.

Their interaction produces outgoing copies, and the number of copies is determined by the number of auxiliary ports on the other agent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001053","sourceNodeId":"n0001053","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"When a replicator meets a fan, the replicator is copied through the fan's two auxiliary ports and the fan is copied through every replicator port.","status":"expanded"} -->

When a replicator meets a fan, the replicator is copied through the fan's two auxiliary ports.

The fan is copied through every port of the replicator.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-bd3e97bee69efaf5","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"wire","status":"expanded"} -->

A wire is a connection represented as an edge between attachment points in a computational graph. It preserves which components are connected and can be redirected when the graph is rewritten.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001032","sourceNodeId":"n0001032","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"An interaction step either removes an active pair or lets it pass through while reconnecting nearby wires.","status":"expanded"} -->

An interaction step acts on an active pair of agents.

The step either removes the active pair or lets it pass through while reconnecting nearby wires.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-da3c108d9a23691e","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"interaction step","status":"expanded"} -->

An interaction step is one local computation in which two connected graph components are replaced according to an interaction rule, meaning the specified replacement and reconnection pattern for that pair. The replacement reconnects the surrounding wires and attachment points.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001034","sourceNodeId":"n0001034","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Perfect confluence gives a one-step diamond: two possible steps from N reach a common net after one more interaction each.","status":"expanded"} -->

Perfect confluence means that alternative interaction choices can be reconciled to a common result.

**Derivation.** Its one-step diamond property says that two possible steps from N reach a common net after one additional interaction from each resulting net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-4d202b7f011a437e","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"graph representation","status":"expanded"} -->

A graph representation describes a computational structure as components joined by connections. Components have named attachment points, and connections show which attachment points are linked, making local rewrites explicit.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001052","sourceNodeId":"n0001052","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Fan annihilation is beta-reduction in graph form.","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan annihilation is the graph-form counterpart of beta-reduction.

The equivalence means that performing fan annihilation in the graph corresponds to carrying out beta-reduction in the represented computation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-bc2f6e263808d0db","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"port identity","status":"expanded"} -->

Port identity is the identity of a particular attachment point on a component or at the boundary of a graph. Preserving that identity during rewriting specifies exactly where each resulting connection remains attached.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001054","sourceNodeId":"n0001054","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Fan-replicator reconnection is determined by port identities: (Fᵢ)ⱼ ↔ (Rʲ)ᵢ.","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan-replicator reconnection is determined by port identities rather than by an arbitrary ordering of the surrounding graph.

The exact port pairing is written (Fᵢ)ⱼ ↔ (Rʲ)ᵢ; the labels identify the ports that are paired without changing the stated notation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a10333847f597672","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"arity","status":"expanded"} -->

Arity is the number of inputs, arguments, or connections that an object or operation accepts.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001047","sourceNodeId":"n0001047","sourceUnitIds":["u000097","u000098","u000099","u000100","u000101","u000102","u000103","u000104","u000105","u000106","u000107","u000108","u000109","u000110","u000111","u000112","u000113","u000114","u000115","u000116","u000117","u000118","u000119","u000120","u000121","u000122","u000123","u000124","u000125","u000126","u000127","u000128","u000129","u000130","u000131","u000132","u000133","u000134","u000135","u000136","u000137","u000138","u000139","u000140","u000141","u000142","u000143","u000144","u000145","u000146","u000147","u000148","u000149","u000150","u000151","u000152","u000153","u000154","u000155","u000156","u000157","u000158","u000159","u000160","u000161","u000162","u000163","u000164","u000165","u000166","u000167","u000168","u000169","u000170","u000171","u000172","u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Equal replicators formally require matching level, arity, and every port delta.","status":"expanded"} -->

Equal replicators must have matching levels.

Equal replicators must have matching arities.

Equal replicators must have matching deltas at every corresponding port.

## Do independent work together

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000137","sourceNodeId":"n0000137","sourceUnitIds":["u000099"],"sourceAnchor":"### Do independent work together","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Do independent work together

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000138","sourceNodeId":"n0000138","sourceUnitIds":["u000100"],"sourceAnchor":"Each agent has one principal port, so two distinct active pairs never share an agent. They can fire at the same time and pick their local rules independently.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Each agent has one principal port, so two distinct active pairs never share an agent. They can fire at the same time and pick their local rules independently.

## 2.3 “Perfect confluence” is the parallelism enabler

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000139","sourceNodeId":"n0000139","sourceUnitIds":["u000101"],"sourceAnchor":"### 2.3 “Perfect confluence” is the parallelism enabler","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 2.3 “Perfect confluence” is the parallelism enabler

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000141","sourceNodeId":"n0000141","sourceUnitIds":["u000103"],"sourceAnchor":"The one-step diamond","status":"source_ready"} -->

The one-step diamond

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000142","sourceNodeId":"n0000142","sourceUnitIds":["u000104"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nN((N))\nN1((N₁))\nN2((N₂))\nM((M))\nN —>|one interaction| N1\nN —>|one interaction| N2\nN1 —>|one interaction| M\nN2 —>|one interaction| M","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
N((N))
N1((N₁))
N2((N₂))
M((M))
N -->|one interaction| N1
N -->|one interaction| N2
N1 -->|one interaction| M
N2 -->|one interaction| M

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000143","sourceNodeId":"n0000143","sourceUnitIds":["u000105"],"sourceAnchor":"How to read the diamond. Starting at N, choose either available step. The paths meet at M. Every arrow shows exactly one local interaction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

How to read the diamond. Starting at N, choose either available step. The paths meet at M. Every arrow shows exactly one local interaction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000145","sourceNodeId":"n0000145","sourceUnitIds":["u000107"],"sourceAnchor":"◇","status":"source_ready"} -->

◇

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000662","sourceNodeId":"n0000662","sourceUnitIds":["u000102"],"sourceAnchor":"Confluence asks whether different valid reduction choices can produce different results.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Confluence asks whether different valid reduction choices can produce different results.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000665","sourceNodeId":"n0000665","sourceUnitIds":["u000106"],"sourceAnchor":"Each active pair owns the only principal port of each participating agent.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Each active pair owns the only principal port of each participating agent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000666","sourceNodeId":"n0000666","sourceUnitIds":["u000106"],"sourceAnchor":"Distinct active pairs therefore use distinct agents.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Distinct active pairs therefore use distinct agents.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-7982b6985cfa36eb","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"one-step interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

A one-step interaction is a process in which one participant or component performs a single action with another participant or component, without describing later actions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-54c727d142ff3548","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"one-step result","status":"expanded"} -->

A one-step result is the immediate outcome produced by one interaction or operation, before any further steps occur.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-078c051d252ead9c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"net","status":"expanded"} -->

A net is a connected system of nodes and links that represents relationships, communication paths, or interactions among its parts.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000663","sourceNodeId":"n0000663","sourceUnitIds":["u000102"],"sourceAnchor":"The one-step diamond property requires either one-step result to reach a common net after one further interaction.","status":"expanded"} -->

The one-step diamond property compares two results produced by taking different one-step interactions.

It requires either one-step result to reach a common net after one further interaction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000140","sourceNodeId":"n0000140","sourceUnitIds":["u000102"],"sourceAnchor":"Confluence asks whether different valid reduction choices can disagree. The one-step diamond property is a strong “no”: if a net N has two possible single steps, either choice leads to a net that meets the other after exactly one more interaction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Confluence asks whether different valid reduction choices can disagree. The one-step diamond property is a strong “no”: if a net N has two possible single steps, either choice leads to a net that meets the other after exactly one more interaction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d442be1e167f0dba","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"rewrite","status":"expanded"} -->

A rewrite is a new version of existing text, code, or other content that preserves or changes its meaning while expressing it differently.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f886c8d7ec0d737c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"nearby wiring","status":"expanded"} -->

Nearby wiring means the local connections that link a component to the other components immediately around it, such as passing data, control signals, or configuration between neighboring steps in a system.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000667","sourceNodeId":"n0000667","sourceUnitIds":["u000106"],"sourceAnchor":"Rewrites of distinct active pairs commute and preserve the same nearby wiring.","status":"expanded"} -->

Rewrites of two distinct active pairs commute, so performing them in either order gives the same result.

The rewrites preserve the same nearby wiring because each rewrite acts on its own distinct active pair.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000144","sourceNodeId":"n0000144","sourceUnitIds":["u000106"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper calls this perfect confluence. Each active pair owns the only principal port of each of its two agents, so distinct active pairs use distinct agents. Their rewrites commute: left-then-right and right-then-left leave the same nearby wiring.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The paper calls this perfect confluence. Each active pair owns the only principal port of each of its two agents, so distinct active pairs use distinct agents. Their rewrites commute: left-then-right and right-then-left leave the same nearby wiring.

## Agreement and termination are separate questions

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000146","sourceNodeId":"n0000146","sourceUnitIds":["u000108"],"sourceAnchor":"### Agreement and termination are separate questions","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Agreement and termination are separate questions

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000669","sourceNodeId":"n0000669","sourceUnitIds":["u000109"],"sourceAnchor":"The diamond property concerns agreement between competing local steps.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The diamond property concerns agreement between competing local steps.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000670","sourceNodeId":"n0000670","sourceUnitIds":["u000109"],"sourceAnchor":"The diamond property does not imply that reduction terminates.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The diamond property does not imply that reduction terminates.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000671","sourceNodeId":"n0000671","sourceUnitIds":["u000109"],"sourceAnchor":"For a normalizing core interaction order, every normalizing order reaches the same result in the same number of interactions.","status":"expanded"} -->

For a normalizing core interaction order, every interaction order that normalizes reaches the same result.

Every such normalizing order reaches that result after the same number of interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000147","sourceNodeId":"n0000147","sourceUnitIds":["u000109"],"sourceAnchor":"The diamond says competing local steps agree. Termination is separate: some nets can reduce forever. The paper’s claim is narrower: when a core interaction order normalizes, every normalizing order reaches the same result in the same number of interactions.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The diamond says competing local steps agree. Termination is separate: some nets can reduce forever. The paper’s claim is narrower: when a core interaction order normalizes, every normalizing order reaches the same result in the same number of interactions.

## 2.4 What earlier optimal-reduction nets struggled with

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000148","sourceNodeId":"n0000148","sourceUnitIds":["u000110"],"sourceAnchor":"### 2.4 What earlier optimal-reduction nets struggled with","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 2.4 What earlier optimal-reduction nets struggled with

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000150","sourceNodeId":"n0000150","sourceUnitIds":["u000112"],"sourceAnchor":"!","status":"source_ready"} -->

!

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000674","sourceNodeId":"n0000674","sourceUnitIds":["u000111"],"sourceAnchor":"Delimiter agents included brackets, croissants, and related constructs.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Delimiter agents included brackets, croissants, and related constructs.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000676","sourceNodeId":"n0000676","sourceUnitIds":["u000111"],"sourceAnchor":"Some earlier systems allowed indices to grow without bound.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Some earlier systems allowed indices to grow without bound.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-57af142ce0350a12","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"delimiter agent","status":"expanded"} -->

A delimiter agent is an agent that uses designated boundary markers, or delimiters, to separate and identify distinct parts of an input or message.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000673","sourceNodeId":"n0000673","sourceUnitIds":["u000111"],"sourceAnchor":"Earlier approaches represented each sharing scope with indexed fans and delimiter agents.","status":"expanded"} -->

Earlier approaches represented each sharing scope with indexed fans.

Those approaches also used delimiter agents as part of the representation for each sharing scope.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-b1b16bc107b7ffca","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"delimiter interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

Delimiter interaction is the way boundary markers, such as quotation marks, brackets, or code fences, affect how surrounding text is grouped and interpreted.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-cd93ed099acd98c3","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fan work","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan work is creative work made by fans that draws on, responds to, or transforms an existing fictional universe, public figure, or media property.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c9cee83d8c8f56ad","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"interaction count","status":"expanded"} -->

An interaction count is the number of recorded interactions involving an entity, such as messages, replies, comments, or other connections.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000675","sourceNodeId":"n0000675","sourceUnitIds":["u000111"],"sourceAnchor":"Delimiter interactions could outnumber the useful fan work.","status":"expanded"} -->

Delimiter interactions could occur more frequently than the useful fan-work interactions.

**Limitation.** Thus, the interaction count could be dominated by delimiter handling rather than by useful fan work.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000149","sourceNodeId":"n0000149","sourceUnitIds":["u000111"],"sourceAnchor":"Earlier interaction-net approaches tracked sharing scopes—regions governed by one duplication context, the way a λ governs its bound uses. They marked those regions with indexed fans plus extra delimiter agents (brackets, croissants, and kin). The critique is practical as much as theoretical: delimiters pile up, and their interactions can outnumber the useful fan work. Some systems also let indices grow without bound.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Earlier interaction-net approaches tracked sharing scopes—regions governed by one duplication context, the way a λ governs its bound uses. They marked those regions with indexed fans plus extra delimiter agents (brackets, croissants, and kin).

The critique is practical as much as theoretical: delimiters pile up, and their interactions can outnumber the useful fan work. Some systems also let indices grow without bound.

## The proposed simplification

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000151","sourceNodeId":"n0000151","sourceUnitIds":["u000113"],"sourceAnchor":"### The proposed simplification","status":"source_ready"} -->

### The proposed simplification

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000152","sourceNodeId":"n0000152","sourceUnitIds":["u000114"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets folds that bookkeeping into one variable-arity agent—the replicator—with a level and a per-port delta. One structure holds the sharing context.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ-Nets folds that bookkeeping into one variable-arity agent—the replicator—with a level and a per-port delta. One structure holds the sharing context.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000153","sourceNodeId":"n0000153","sourceUnitIds":["u000115"],"sourceAnchor":"03 Core vocabulary","status":"source_ready"} -->

03 Core vocabulary

## Three agents carry the whole core.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000154","sourceNodeId":"n0000154","sourceUnitIds":["u000116"],"sourceAnchor":"## Three agents carry the whole core.","status":"source_ready"} -->

## Three agents carry the whole core.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000155","sourceNodeId":"n0000155","sourceUnitIds":["u000117"],"sourceAnchor":"A Δ-Net is built from three agents: fan, eraser, and replicator. Think of each as a tiny component. The principal port is the socket that can start a rewrite. The auxiliary ports carry the surrounding wiring through that rewrite.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A Δ-Net is built from three agents: fan, eraser, and replicator. Think of each as a tiny component.

The principal port is the socket that can start a rewrite. The auxiliary ports carry the surrounding wiring through that rewrite.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000156","sourceNodeId":"n0000156","sourceUnitIds":["u000118"],"sourceAnchor":"Fan · 2 auxiliary ports","status":"source_ready"} -->

Fan · 2 auxiliary ports

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000157","sourceNodeId":"n0000157","sourceUnitIds":["u000119"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nFp[\"principal\"]\nF@{ shape: triangle, label: \"F\" }\nFa0@{ shape: rect, label: \"1st aux\" }\nFa1@{ shape: rect, label: \"2nd aux\" }\nFp —>|principal| F\nF —- Fa0\nF —- Fa1","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
Fp["principal"]
F@{ shape: triangle, label: "F" }
Fa0@{ shape: rect, label: "1st aux" }
Fa1@{ shape: rect, label: "2nd aux" }
Fp -->|principal| F
F --- Fa0
F --- Fa1

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000158","sourceNodeId":"n0000158","sourceUnitIds":["u000120"],"sourceAnchor":"Eraser · no auxiliary ports","status":"source_ready"} -->

Eraser · no auxiliary ports

Replicator · any number of auxiliary ports

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000163","sourceNodeId":"n0000163","sourceUnitIds":["u000125"],"sourceAnchor":"Structure","status":"source_ready"} -->

Structure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000679","sourceNodeId":"n0000679","sourceUnitIds":["u000124"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicators are trapezoids with a centered top principal port and evenly spaced bottom auxiliary ports.","status":"expanded"} -->

Replicators are represented as trapezoids.

Each replicator has a centered principal port at the top and evenly spaced auxiliary ports along the bottom.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000680","sourceNodeId":"n0000680","sourceUnitIds":["u000124"],"sourceAnchor":"Rectangles denote fragments or wire endpoints rather than agents.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Rectangles denote fragments or wire endpoints rather than agents.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000681","sourceNodeId":"n0000681","sourceUnitIds":["u000124"],"sourceAnchor":"An eraser has only a principal port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An eraser has only a principal port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-31acc1481df1ab2f","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"application fan","status":"expanded"} -->

An application fan is the number or pattern of application components that depend directly on, call, or use a particular component, service, or interface. A high application fan indicates broad reuse and means that changes to the shared component may affect many parts of the application.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-209163225799547c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"function fan","status":"expanded"} -->

A function fan describes the number or pattern of a function’s connections in a call graph; the specific direction must be stated, because fan-in counts callers while fan-out counts functions it calls.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-998fbadb1fba0588","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"auxiliary position","status":"expanded"} -->

An auxiliary position is a structural location in a sentence or formal representation occupied by an auxiliary element, such as a helping verb, rather than by the main lexical predicate.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000678","sourceNodeId":"n0000678","sourceUnitIds":["u000124"],"sourceAnchor":"Application and function fans are triangles with a top principal port and two fixed bottom auxiliary positions.","status":"expanded"} -->

Application fans and function fans are represented as triangles.

Each triangle has a top principal port and two fixed auxiliary positions at the bottom.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000162","sourceNodeId":"n0000162","sourceUnitIds":["u000124"],"sourceAnchor":"Diagram key. Application and function fans are triangles: the principal port is fixed at the top apex and the two auxiliary wires leave fixed, symmetric positions along the bottom edge. Only replicators are trapezoids: the principal port is centered on the short top side and the auxiliary wires are evenly spaced along the wide bottom side. Rectangles label fragments or wire endpoints; they are not agents. An eraser has only a principal port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Diagram key. Application and function fans are triangles: the principal port is fixed at the top apex and the two auxiliary wires leave fixed, symmetric positions along the bottom edge.

Only replicators are trapezoids: the principal port is centered on the short top side and the auxiliary wires are evenly spaced along the wide bottom side. Rectangles label fragments or wire endpoints; they are not agents.

An eraser has only a principal port.

## Fan

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000164","sourceNodeId":"n0000164","sourceUnitIds":["u000126"],"sourceAnchor":"### Fan","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Fan

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000165","sourceNodeId":"n0000165","sourceUnitIds":["u000127"],"sourceAnchor":"Exactly two auxiliary ports. Fans build the application and abstraction skeleton of a λ-term. When two fans annihilate, that is β-reduction in graph form.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Exactly two auxiliary ports. Fans build the application and abstraction skeleton of a λ-term. When two fans annihilate, that is β-reduction in graph form.
