A fan is a structural agent with two auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000479","sourceNodeId":"n0000479","sourceUnitIds":["u000422"],"sourceAnchor":"In a rooted net, a fan can encode either an application or an abstraction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000167","sourceNodeId":"n0000167","sourceUnitIds":["u000129"],"sourceAnchor":"### Eraser","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Eraser

No auxiliary ports. It marks “this bound variable is unused,” and deletes an agent it meets in a distinct-agent interaction.

## Replicator

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000170","sourceNodeId":"n0000170","sourceUnitIds":["u000132"],"sourceAnchor":"### Replicator","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Replicator

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000172","sourceNodeId":"n0000172","sourceUnitIds":["u000134"],"sourceAnchor":"Context","status":"source_ready"} -->

Context

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000488","sourceNodeId":"n0000488","sourceUnitIds":["u000427"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicator","status":"source_ready"} -->

Replicator

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000489","sourceNodeId":"n0000489","sourceUnitIds":["u000428"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator is a variable-arity agent used for sharing.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A replicator is a variable-arity agent used for sharing.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000490","sourceNodeId":"n0000490","sourceUnitIds":["u000428"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator has a level and an integer delta for each auxiliary port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A replicator has a level and an integer delta for each auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000683","sourceNodeId":"n0000683","sourceUnitIds":["u000133"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator may have any natural-number arity.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A replicator may have any natural-number arity.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000684","sourceNodeId":"n0000684","sourceUnitIds":["u000133"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator joins the uses of one shared value.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A replicator joins the uses of one shared value.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000685","sourceNodeId":"n0000685","sourceUnitIds":["u000133"],"sourceAnchor":"The replicator stores a sharing context as a level on the agent and a delta on each auxiliary port.","status":"expanded"} -->

The replicator stores a sharing context using a level attached to the agent itself.

It also stores a delta separately on each auxiliary port. The sharing context therefore has one agent-level value and a port-specific value for every auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000171","sourceNodeId":"n0000171","sourceUnitIds":["u000133"],"sourceAnchor":"Any natural-number arity—one, two, three auxiliary ports, and so on. It joins the uses of one shared value and records the sharing context: a level on the agent, plus a delta on each auxiliary port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Any natural-number arity—one, two, three auxiliary ports, and so on. It joins the uses of one shared value and records the sharing context: a level on the agent, plus a delta on each auxiliary port.

## Level delta

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000173","sourceNodeId":"n0000173","sourceUnitIds":["u000135"],"sourceAnchor":"### Level delta","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Level delta

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000174","sourceNodeId":"n0000174","sourceUnitIds":["u000136"],"sourceAnchor":"The integer level names the replicator’s sharing context. Each auxiliary port carries an integer delta. A replica that leaves through a port adds that delta to its current level—like a signed offset stamped on the exit door.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The integer level names the replicator’s sharing context. Each auxiliary port carries an integer delta. A replica that leaves through a port adds that delta to its current level—like a signed offset stamped on the exit door.

## 3.1 Four systems fall out of the same pieces

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000175","sourceNodeId":"n0000175","sourceUnitIds":["u000137"],"sourceAnchor":"### 3.1 Four systems fall out of the same pieces","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 3.1 Four systems fall out of the same pieces

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000176","sourceNodeId":"n0000176","sourceUnitIds":["u000138"],"sourceAnchor":"System | Agents permitted | Corresponding term fragment | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

System | Agents permitted | Corresponding term fragment | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000177","sourceNodeId":"n0000177","sourceUnitIds":["u000139"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔL | Fan | Linear: exactly one use. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

ΔL | Fan | Linear: exactly one use. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000178","sourceNodeId":"n0000178","sourceUnitIds":["u000140"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔA | Fan + eraser | Affine: a use may be absent. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

ΔA | Fan + eraser | Affine: a use may be absent. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000179","sourceNodeId":"n0000179","sourceUnitIds":["u000141"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔI | Fan + replicator | Relevant: a use may repeat. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

ΔI | Fan + replicator | Relevant: a use may repeat. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000180","sourceNodeId":"n0000180","sourceUnitIds":["u000142"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔK | Fan + eraser + replicator | Full: absence and repetition are both allowed. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

ΔK | Fan + eraser + replicator | Full: absence and repetition are both allowed. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000687","sourceNodeId":"n0000687","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"Linear terms use each bound variable exactly once.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Linear terms use each bound variable exactly once.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000688","sourceNodeId":"n0000688","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"Affine terms use each bound variable at most once.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Affine terms use each bound variable at most once.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000689","sourceNodeId":"n0000689","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"Relevant terms use each bound variable at least once.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Relevant terms use each bound variable at least once.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000690","sourceNodeId":"n0000690","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"Full terms permit any number of uses.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Full terms permit any number of uses.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000691","sourceNodeId":"n0000691","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"Fans encode structure, erasers encode omission, and replicators encode sharing.","status":"expanded"} -->

To encode something means to represent information or meaning through a particular form or notation.

Fans encode structure.

Erasers encode omission.

Replicators encode sharing.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000181","sourceNodeId":"n0000181","sourceUnitIds":["u000143"],"sourceAnchor":"In plain language: linear means exactly one bound use, affine at most one, relevant at least one, and full any count. The split mirrors the paper’s opening taxonomy and shows which agent pays for which feature: fans for structure, erasers for omission, replicators for sharing.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In plain language: linear means exactly one bound use, affine at most one, relevant at least one, and full any count. The split mirrors the paper’s opening taxonomy and shows which agent pays for which feature: fans for structure, erasers for omission, replicators for sharing.

## 3.2 Equality is deliberately local in practice

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000182","sourceNodeId":"n0000182","sourceUnitIds":["u000144"],"sourceAnchor":"### 3.2 Equality is deliberately local in practice","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 3.2 Equality is deliberately local in practice

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000184","sourceNodeId":"n0000184","sourceUnitIds":["u000146"],"sourceAnchor":"What does “fan-in” versus “fan-out” mean here?","status":"source_ready"} -->

What does “fan-in” versus “fan-out” mean here?

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000186","sourceNodeId":"n0000186","sourceUnitIds":["u000148"],"sourceAnchor":"04 Local dynamics","status":"source_ready"} -->

04 Local dynamics

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000692","sourceNodeId":"n0000692","sourceUnitIds":["u000145"],"sourceAnchor":"Equal replicators must match in level, arity, and every port delta.","status":"expanded"} -->

Equal replicators must have the same level and the same arity.

Equal replicators must also have matching port deltas at every port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000694","sourceNodeId":"n0000694","sourceUnitIds":["u000145"],"sourceAnchor":"Hand-built nets cannot compare replicator levels alone.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Hand-built nets cannot compare replicator levels alone.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000696","sourceNodeId":"n0000696","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"Parent and child are orientation labels on a wire relative to the root.","status":"expanded"} -->

A parent and a child are orientation labels assigned to the two ends of a wire; they describe the wire's direction relative to the root of the structure.

The labels do not describe different kinds of wire; they indicate which endpoint is closer to the root and which endpoint is farther from it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000697","sourceNodeId":"n0000697","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonical nets begin with unpaired fan-in replicators whose auxiliaries are parents and principal is a child.","status":"expanded"} -->

A canonical net starts with a fan-in replicator that has no paired counterpart, so the replicator is called unpaired.

In an unpaired fan-in replicator, the auxiliary ports are oriented as parents and the principal port is oriented as a child.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000700","sourceNodeId":"n0000700","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"Levels and deltas provide local evidence for pairing.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Levels and deltas provide local evidence for pairing.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-b3b76deafd37c659","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fan-out","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan-out is the number of distinct destinations or branches that originate from one source node, component, or process.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-bbbfd6e4c2c961b9","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"orientation","status":"expanded"} -->

Orientation is the arrangement or direction of something relative to a chosen reference point, coordinate system, or perspective.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000698","sourceNodeId":"n0000698","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"Reduction can create fan-outs with the opposite orientation.","status":"expanded"} -->

Reduction can create fan-outs with the opposite orientation.

The fan-outs introduced by reduction need not retain the relevant orientation; they can have the opposite orientation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-548037a2a2bb8c30","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fan-in","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan-in is the number of distinct inputs, dependencies, or upstream components that provide data or control to a component.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-e6311be65a1899e5","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"upstream","status":"expanded"} -->

Upstream refers to a component, process, or event that occurs earlier in a dependency or data-flow chain and provides inputs to a downstream component.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-0ee1c030f134d1c9","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"partner","status":"expanded"} -->

A partner is a person, group, or organization that works with another person or group toward a shared goal.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000699","sourceNodeId":"n0000699","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"A fan-out is paired upstream with at least one fan-in, but a fan-in need not have a partner.","status":"expanded"} -->

A fan-out is a pattern in which one element connects to multiple elements, while a fan-in is a pattern in which multiple elements connect to one element.

A fan-out is paired upstream with at least one fan-in.

**Limitation.** The pairing is not required in the opposite direction: a fan-in may have no fan-out partner.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000185","sourceNodeId":"n0000185","sourceUnitIds":["u000147"],"sourceAnchor":"Parent and child are direction labels on a wire. In a rooted net, a parent points toward the root and a child points away from it—think “who faces whom,” not family trees. Canonical nets start with unpaired fan-in replicators: auxiliaries are parents, principal is a child. Reduction can also create fan-outs, with that orientation flipped. A fan-out is paired upstream with at least one fan-in; a fan-in need not have a partner. Levels and deltas let you reason about that pairing locally.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Parent and child are direction labels on a wire. In a rooted net, a parent points toward the root and a child points away from it—think “who faces whom,” not family trees.

Canonical nets start with unpaired fan-in replicators: auxiliaries are parents, principal is a child. Reduction can also create fan-outs, with that orientation flipped.

A fan-out is paired upstream with at least one fan-in; a fan-in need not have a partner. Levels and deltas let you reason about that pairing locally.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6c5bf70e9fae5f69","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"the meaning of a well-formed λ-term translation","status":"expanded"} -->

A well-formed λ-term translation follows the formation rules of lambda calculus: a λ-term is built from variables, abstractions, and applications in a syntactically valid arrangement.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-b86e28177afa1f58","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"the definition of an active replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

An active replicator is a replicator node whose principal port is connected to another agent’s principal port, so that a local interaction rule can apply.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000693","sourceNodeId":"n0000693","sourceUnitIds":["u000145"],"sourceAnchor":"Well-formed λ-term translations make same-level active replicators equal.","status":"expanded"} -->

A λ-term is an expression in the lambda calculus, and a translation maps it into another formal representation.

The translations in this claim are well-formed translations of λ-terms.

In well-formed λ-term translations, active replicators at the same structural level are equal.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000183","sourceNodeId":"n0000183","sourceUnitIds":["u000145"],"sourceAnchor":"Formally, equal replicators must match in level, arity, and every port delta. For well-formed nets from the paper’s λ-term translation, active replicators at the same level are already equal—so an implementation may compare levels alone in that setting. Hand-built nets do not get that shortcut.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Formally, equal replicators must match in level, arity, and every port delta. For well-formed nets from the paper’s λ-term translation, active replicators at the same level are already equal—so an implementation may compare levels alone in that setting. Hand-built nets do not get that shortcut.

## When two principal ports touch, one of three things happens.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000187","sourceNodeId":"n0000187","sourceUnitIds":["u000149"],"sourceAnchor":"## When two principal ports touch, one of three things happens.","status":"source_ready"} -->

## When two principal ports touch, one of three things happens.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000188","sourceNodeId":"n0000188","sourceUnitIds":["u000150"],"sourceAnchor":"The interaction rules are the engine. This chapter groups their wire reconnections into three families: cancellation, deletion, and passage-with-copying. Each name describes what happens to one active pair.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The interaction rules are the engine. This chapter groups their wire reconnections into three families: cancellation, deletion, and passage-with-copying. Each name describes what happens to one active pair.

## 1. Annihilation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000189","sourceNodeId":"n0000189","sourceUnitIds":["u000151"],"sourceAnchor":"### 1. Annihilation","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 1. Annihilation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000702","sourceNodeId":"n0000702","sourceUnitIds":["u000152"],"sourceAnchor":"Equal agents annihilate each other.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Equal agents annihilate each other.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-74c4bd17b9109ba0","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"active replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

An active replicator is a replicating entity that actively participates in producing copies of itself.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c7288ddeac130052","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fan annihilation","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan annihilation is the local interaction in which a matched pair of fan or replicator nodes disappears and corresponding auxiliary ports reconnect. At equal levels, matching port indices reconnect while the lower node’s rotated ordering makes the outer wires cross.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-4ded140729c2919a","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"λ abstraction","status":"expanded"} -->

A λ abstraction is a function expression written with the Greek letter λ, a parameter, and a body, such as λx. x + 1; it represents a function that takes x as input and returns x + 1.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c569f81d30deecef","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"argument","status":"expanded"} -->

An argument is a value or expression supplied to a function when the function is called, such as 3 in the call f(3).

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000704","sourceNodeId":"n0000704","sourceUnitIds":["u000152"],"sourceAnchor":"Fan annihilation represents applying a λ abstraction to its argument.","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan annihilation is an interaction in the graph representation of λ-calculus.

That interaction represents applying a λ abstraction to its argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-3dc4b3e6d370a130","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"lambda calculus","status":"expanded"} -->

The lambda calculus is a formal system for representing computation using functions: expressions can define functions, apply functions to arguments, and substitute arguments into function bodies.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6b1045c38008e34d","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"well-formed λ translation","status":"expanded"} -->

A well-formed λ translation is a lambda-calculus expression that correctly represents the intended meaning while obeying the syntax and variable-binding rules of lambda calculus.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000703","sourceNodeId":"n0000703","sourceUnitIds":["u000152"],"sourceAnchor":"In a well-formed λ translation, same-level active replicators annihilate and matching auxiliary ports reconnect.","status":"expanded"} -->

In a well-formed λ translation, active replicators at the same level annihilate.

After the same-level replicators annihilate, matching auxiliary ports reconnect.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000190","sourceNodeId":"n0000190","sourceUnitIds":["u000152"],"sourceAnchor":"Equal agents cancel. In a well-formed net from a λ-term, active replicators at the same level are equal: both nodes disappear and matching auxiliary ports reconnect. For fans, that same cancellation is applying a λ to its argument.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Equal agents cancel. In a well-formed net from a λ-term, active replicators at the same level are equal: both nodes disappear and matching auxiliary ports reconnect. For fans, that same cancellation is applying a λ to its argument.

## 2. Erasure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000191","sourceNodeId":"n0000191","sourceUnitIds":["u000153"],"sourceAnchor":"### 2. Erasure","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 2. Erasure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000192","sourceNodeId":"n0000192","sourceUnitIds":["u000154"],"sourceAnchor":"An eraser is distinct from its neighbour and has zero auxiliary ports, so the agent it meets has nowhere to continue through. It is deleted.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An eraser is distinct from its neighbour and has zero auxiliary ports, so the agent it meets has nowhere to continue through. It is deleted.

## 3. Commutation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000193","sourceNodeId":"n0000193","sourceUnitIds":["u000155"],"sourceAnchor":"### 3. Commutation","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 3. Commutation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000194","sourceNodeId":"n0000194","sourceUnitIds":["u000156"],"sourceAnchor":"Distinct non-eraser agents pass through each other. How many outgoing copies you get depends on the other agent’s auxiliary ports.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Distinct non-eraser agents pass through each other. How many outgoing copies you get depends on the other agent’s auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000195","sourceNodeId":"n0000195","sourceUnitIds":["u000157"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nE@{ shape: stadium, label: \"E\" }\nF@{ shape: triangle, label: \"F\" }\nM@{ shape: rect, label: \"subnet M\" }\nN@{ shape: rect, label: \"subnet N\" }\nE —>|\"principal ↔ principal\"| F\nF —- M\nF —- N","status":"source_ready"} -->

F --- M
F --- N

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000196","sourceNodeId":"n0000196","sourceUnitIds":["u000158"],"sourceAnchor":"After: E reaches both branches","status":"source_ready"} -->

After: E reaches both branches

E2["E"]

E2 --> N2

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000706","sourceNodeId":"n0000706","sourceUnitIds":["u000160"],"sourceAnchor":"An eraser passes through one auxiliary port of a distinct agent at a time.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An eraser passes through one auxiliary port of a distinct agent at a time.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-cbf39bb861a13e04","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"two-port fan","status":"expanded"} -->

A two-port fan is a device with two fluid connections: one port where air or another fluid enters and one port where it exits. By adding energy to the fluid, the fan creates a pressure difference that drives flow from the inlet toward the outlet.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d344c625346500b8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"eraser","status":"expanded"} -->

An eraser is a material or tool used to remove marks made by a pencil or similar medium. Rubbing it against the marked surface transfers or abrades away the marking material.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000707","sourceNodeId":"n0000707","sourceUnitIds":["u000160"],"sourceAnchor":"A two-port fan meeting an eraser leaves two erasers connected to its auxiliary outputs.","status":"expanded"} -->

A two-port fan can meet an eraser in an interaction.

After this interaction, two erasers remain connected to the fan’s auxiliary outputs.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-59f7c0b28e74de4d","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"replicator output","status":"expanded"} -->

Replicator output is the result produced by a system that copies, reproduces, or generates another object, process, or pattern.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000708","sourceNodeId":"n0000708","sourceUnitIds":["u000160"],"sourceAnchor":"An n-port replicator meeting an eraser leaves n erasers.","status":"expanded"} -->

An n-port replicator can meet an eraser in an interaction.

After this interaction, an eraser remains at each of the replicator’s n outputs, leaving n erasers.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000198","sourceNodeId":"n0000198","sourceUnitIds":["u000160"],"sourceAnchor":"Why erasure propagates. Distinct agents pass through one another once per auxiliary port. Because E has zero auxiliary ports, the fan produces zero surviving copies; because the fan has two auxiliary ports, two copies of E continue into M and N. An n-port replicator would analogously leave n erasers. This is the erasure family from [the paper’s core interaction rules](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4).","status":"source_ready"} -->

Why erasure propagates. Distinct agents pass through one another once per auxiliary port.

Because E has zero auxiliary ports, the fan produces zero surviving copies; because the fan has two auxiliary ports, two copies of E continue into M and N. An n-port replicator would analogously leave n erasers.

This is the erasure family from [the paper’s core interaction rules](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4).

## 4.1 Fan meets fan: the familiar redex disappears

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000199","sourceNodeId":"n0000199","sourceUnitIds":["u000161"],"sourceAnchor":"### 4.1 Fan meets fan: the familiar redex disappears","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
res2@{ shape: rect, label: "result · 1st" }
arg2@{ shape: rect, label: "argument · 2nd" }
body2@{ shape: rect, label: "body · 1st" }
var2@{ shape: rect, label: "variable · 2nd" }
res2 -->|1st ↔ 1st| body2
arg2 -->|2nd ↔ 2nd| var2

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000710","sourceNodeId":"n0000710","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"Applications and abstractions use the same triangular fan shape.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Applications and abstractions use the same triangular fan shape.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000713","sourceNodeId":"n0000713","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"Auxiliary ports are ordered clockwise when the principal port points down.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Auxiliary ports are ordered clockwise when the principal port points down.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000714","sourceNodeId":"n0000714","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"The fan interaction represents β-reduction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The fan interaction represents β-reduction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000716","sourceNodeId":"n0000716","sourceUnitIds":["u000166"],"sourceAnchor":"The downward application fan orders its auxiliaries left-to-right as first and second.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The downward application fan orders its auxiliaries left-to-right as first and second.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-b3534b6b9ef2e897","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"expression use","status":"expanded"} -->

Expression use means placing an expression in a context where its evaluated result is needed, such as assigning it to a variable, passing it as an argument, or using it in a condition.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-601682bc1c4b93c8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"normalizing interaction order","status":"expanded"} -->

Normalizing interaction order means applying a consistent sequence to interactions between components or participants, so equivalent interactions follow the same ordering convention.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-299515557436ab69","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"normalizing order","status":"expanded"} -->

Normalizing order means converting different representations of an ordering into a consistent standard form so that items can be compared or arranged reliably.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-633ae498da6c86da","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"result","status":"expanded"} -->

A result is the outcome produced by a calculation, process, experiment, or argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-165ec9ddedb840ac","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

An interaction is a situation in which two or more things affect one another, so that the outcome depends on their relationship rather than on either one acting alone.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-659fd4f9402d3331","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"indexed fan","status":"expanded"} -->

An indexed fan is a collection of related elements or connections arranged around a central element, with each branch identified by an index so that individual branches can be referenced precisely.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-fdcdc9086d8f9671","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"wire orientation","status":"expanded"} -->

Wire orientation is the direction assigned to a wire in a circuit or network, usually from a designated source end toward a designated destination end.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6bbee6c5cad422c8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"root","status":"expanded"} -->

A root is the starting point of a graph or hierarchical structure; other elements are reached by following connections outward from it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-93fecfd994d05d5b","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"graph element role","status":"expanded"} -->

A graph element role describes what a node or other graph element represents or does within the graph, such as a statement, definition, prerequisite, or evidence item.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000711","sourceNodeId":"n0000711","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"Their roles are identified by wire orientation relative to the root.","status":"expanded"} -->

Wire orientation gives the direction of a wire relative to the graph’s root.

The roles of the graph elements are identified from that orientation relative to the root.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-bd6d9f96545b3410","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fan interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

Fan interaction is the way fans communicate with, respond to, and influence one another around a shared person, team, work, or community.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f80cad9e237894ad","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"auxiliary port matching","status":"expanded"} -->

Auxiliary port matching is the process of establishing correspondence between an auxiliary interface and the interface or function it serves, so that their inputs, outputs, or roles align.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000712","sourceNodeId":"n0000712","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"Principal-to-principal fan interaction removes both fans and reconnects matching auxiliary ports.","status":"expanded"} -->

A principal-to-principal fan interaction removes both participating fans.

It then reconnects auxiliary ports that match one another.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000200","sourceNodeId":"n0000200","sourceUnitIds":["u000162"],"sourceAnchor":"Applications and abstractions are both fans. You tell them apart by how their wires point when you look from the root—the unique top entry of the whole net. When the right pair of fans meets principal-to-principal, they annihilate: both nodes go away and matching auxiliary ports reconnect. Auxiliary ports are ordered clockwise when the principal port points down; flipping one fan upside down swaps its visible left and right, so the wires cross in the drawing. That is β-reduction at the net level.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Applications and abstractions are both fans. You tell them apart by how their wires point when you look from the root—the unique top entry of the whole net.

When the right pair of fans meets principal-to-principal, they annihilate: both nodes go away and matching auxiliary ports reconnect. Auxiliary ports are ordered clockwise when the principal port points down; flipping one fan upside down swaps its visible left and right, so the wires cross in the drawing.

That is β-reduction at the net level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-ae6bb90f7da29a75","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"upward-rotated abstraction fan","status":"expanded"} -->

**Prerequisite.** An upward-rotated abstraction fan is a pattern in which one specific concept connects upward to several broader or more abstract concepts, like the spokes of a fan rotated toward higher levels of generality.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-fd37daa3aed82649","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"auxiliary port order","status":"expanded"} -->

Auxiliary port order is the fixed sequence used to distinguish or identify auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c08d4b1da7805f5c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"first auxiliary","status":"expanded"} -->

An auxiliary is an additional person, object, or concept introduced to help explain, analyze, or solve a problem. The first auxiliary is the earliest such supporting element introduced in a sequence.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-42fffc3657069f75","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"second auxiliary","status":"expanded"} -->

A second auxiliary is the additional helping verb used in a verb phrase after a first auxiliary, such as “have” in “will have finished.”

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000717","sourceNodeId":"n0000717","sourceUnitIds":["u000166"],"sourceAnchor":"The rotated upward abstraction fan orders its auxiliaries right-to-left as first and second.","status":"expanded"} -->

The upward-rotated abstraction fan has auxiliary ports with a specified right-to-left order.

In that order, the first auxiliary is on the right and the second auxiliary is to its left.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-55d5815d764e39b6","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"semantic port index","status":"expanded"} -->

A semantic port index is an index that identifies a connection point by the meaning or type of information it carries, rather than only by its physical position or numeric identifier.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-db95a1b9878fd934","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"matching semantic port indices","status":"expanded"} -->

Matching semantic port indices are identifiers that refer to equivalent or compatible information-carrying connection points, allowing corresponding ports to be paired or connected.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-73574120ccb1cc4c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"drawn wire","status":"expanded"} -->

A drawn wire is a line explicitly shown connecting two ports or connection points in a diagram or net model. It represents a direct connection between those endpoints.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000718","sourceNodeId":"n0000718","sourceUnitIds":["u000166"],"sourceAnchor":"Matching semantic port indices can therefore cross the drawn wires.","status":"expanded"} -->

Semantic port indices identify corresponding ports by their roles in the represented structure.

Consequently, connections for matching semantic port indices may correspond to wires that cross in the drawing.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000204","sourceNodeId":"n0000204","sourceUnitIds":["u000166"],"sourceAnchor":"The crossing is structural. For the downward application fan, the left auxiliary is first and the right auxiliary is second. For the rotated upward abstraction fan, the right auxiliary is first and the left auxiliary is second. Matching first-to-first and second-to-second—not geometric sides—therefore crosses the wires.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The crossing is structural. For the downward application fan, the left auxiliary is first and the right auxiliary is second.

For the rotated upward abstraction fan, the right auxiliary is first and the left auxiliary is second. Matching first-to-first and second-to-second—not geometric sides—therefore crosses the wires.

## 4.2 Replicator meets fan: structure splits around sharing

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000205","sourceNodeId":"n0000205","sourceUnitIds":["u000167"],"sourceAnchor":"### 4.2 Replicator meets fan: structure splits around sharing","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 4.2 Replicator meets fan: structure splits around sharing

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000207","sourceNodeId":"n0000207","sourceUnitIds":["u000169"],"sourceAnchor":"Before: R meets F","status":"source_ready"} -->

Before: R meets F

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000208","sourceNodeId":"n0000208","sourceUnitIds":["u000170"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nd0@{ shape: rect, label: \"d₀\" }\nd1@{ shape: rect, label: \"d₁\" }\nd2@{ shape: rect, label: \"d₂\" }\nR@{ shape: trapezoid, label: \"R\" }\nF@{ shape: triangle, label: \"F\" }\na1@{ shape: rect, label: \"a₁\" }\na0@{ shape: rect, label: \"a₀\" }\nd0 —- R\nd1 —- R\nd2 —- R\nR —>|\"principal ↔ principal\"| F\nF —- a1\nF —- a0","status":"source_ready"} -->

After: (Fᵢ)ⱼ ↔ (Rʲ)ᵢ

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000211","sourceNodeId":"n0000211","sourceUnitIds":["u000173"],"sourceAnchor":"Finite version of the paper’s fan–replicator rule. Port identities, not geometric sides, determine the reconnection: (Fᵢ)ⱼ ↔ (Rʲ)ᵢ. This guide-specific diagram uses a three-port replicator so every copy has a visible port and wire. It adapts the structure of [Figure 4 in the source paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4) (CC BY 4.0).","status":"source_ready"} -->

Finite version of the paper’s fan–replicator rule. Port identities, not geometric sides, determine the reconnection: (Fᵢ)ⱼ ↔ (Rʲ)ᵢ.

This guide-specific diagram uses a three-port replicator so every copy has a visible port and wire. It adapts the structure of [Figure 4 in the source paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4) (CC BY 4.0).

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000213","sourceNodeId":"n0000213","sourceUnitIds":["u000174"],"sourceAnchor":"Count both sides. R has three auxiliary ports, so three fans F₀, F₁, F₂ appear. F has two auxiliary ports, so two replicators R⁰, R¹ appear. Because F points upward, its clockwise auxiliary-port order is visible right-to-left: a₁ is on the left and a₀ is on the right. Thus the old visible-left a₁ wire remains attached to R¹, while the old visible-right a₀ wire remains attached to R⁰; those two outer wires do not cross. Internally, auxiliary port j of each new Fᵢ connects to port dᵢ of Rʲ.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Count both sides. R has three auxiliary ports, so three fans F₀, F₁, F₂ appear.

F has two auxiliary ports, so two replicators R⁰, R¹ appear. Because F points upward, its clockwise auxiliary-port order is visible right-to-left: a₁ is on the left and a₀ is on the right.

Thus the old visible-left a₁ wire remains attached to R¹, while the old visible-right a₀ wire remains attached to R⁰; those two outer wires do not cross. Internally, auxiliary port j of each new Fᵢ connects to port dᵢ of Rʲ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000720","sourceNodeId":"n0000720","sourceUnitIds":["u000168"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator meeting a fan produces one exact fan copy per replicator auxiliary port.","status":"expanded"} -->

When a replicator meets a fan, the replicator makes an exact copy of that fan at each auxiliary port.

The number of exact fan copies produced equals the number of auxiliary ports on the replicator: one copy per port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000721","sourceNodeId":"n0000721","sourceUnitIds":["u000168"],"sourceAnchor":"The fan produces one fan per auxiliary port of the replicator.","status":"expanded"} -->

A fan connected to a replicator produces one fan for every auxiliary port on that replicator.

Thus, the replicator's auxiliary ports determine the fan's output count, with each port corresponding to one produced fan.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000722","sourceNodeId":"n0000722","sourceUnitIds":["u000168"],"sourceAnchor":"Each resulting agent retains its original role in the other agent’s surrounding context.","status":"expanded"} -->

Each resulting agent retains its original role.

That retained role applies within the other agent’s surrounding context.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000206","sourceNodeId":"n0000206","sourceUnitIds":["u000168"],"sourceAnchor":"When a replicator meets a fan, the replicator leaves through the fan’s two auxiliary ports as two exact copies. At the same time, the fan leaves through every auxiliary port of the replicator—one fan per port. Each keeps its role, but now sits in the other’s surrounding context.","status":"source_ready"} -->

When a replicator meets a fan, the replicator leaves through the fan’s two auxiliary ports as two exact copies. At the same time, the fan leaves through every auxiliary port of the replicator—one fan per port. Each keeps its role, but now sits in the other’s surrounding context.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001056","sourceNodeId":"n0001056","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicators at different levels commute rather than annihilate.","status":"expanded"} -->

Replicators at different levels do not annihilate because their levels do not match.

Instead, replicators at different levels commute with one another.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001058","sourceNodeId":"n0001058","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"Each H replica's new level equals H's old level plus the delta on the R port it exits.","status":"expanded"} -->

Each H replica exits through one auxiliary port of R, and that R port supplies the delta used in the level update.

**Derivation.** If H's old level is L and the delta on the R port used by a replica is Δ, that replica's new level is L + Δ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001060","sourceNodeId":"n0001060","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"During different-level replicator commutation, auxiliary port j of each H replica connects to the matching port of Rʲ.","status":"expanded"} -->

In different-level replicator commutation, each replica H has auxiliary ports identified by labels such as j.

For every H replica, its auxiliary port j connects to the port with the same label on Rʲ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001061","sourceNodeId":"n0001061","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"A port delta is an integer local level adjustment applied to every higher-level replica exiting through that port.","status":"expanded"} -->

A port delta is an integer that specifies a local adjustment to a replica’s level at a particular port.

The port delta is applied to every higher-level replica that exits through that port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001062","sourceNodeId":"n0001062","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"For R at level 2, an endpoint at level 2 gives delta 0 and an endpoint at level 5 gives delta +3.","status":"expanded"} -->

For R operating at level 2, an endpoint is also at level 2 when the level difference, called delta, is 0.

For the same R at level 2, an endpoint at level 5 produces delta +3 because level 5 is three levels above level 2.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001063","sourceNodeId":"n0001063","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"An H replica at level 7 exits through delta 0 at level 7 and through delta +3 at level 10.","status":"expanded"} -->

An H replica operating at level 7 exits through delta 0 when the exit level is also 7.

The same H replica exits through delta +3 when the exit level is 10, because level 10 is three levels above level 7.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001064","sourceNodeId":"n0001064","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"A delta may be negative, zero, or positive.","status":"expanded"} -->

A delta may have a negative value or a positive value.

A delta may also have a value of zero.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001065","sourceNodeId":"n0001065","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"For each lambda fragment, the translation φ is a bijection between terms and canonical Δ-Nets.","status":"expanded"} -->

For each lambda fragment, φ maps every term to exactly one canonical Δ-Net.

For each lambda fragment, every canonical Δ-Net is the φ-translation of exactly one term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001066","sourceNodeId":"n0001066","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"The translation is inductive: translate smaller term fragments first and then wire them into larger fragments.","status":"expanded"} -->

The translation proceeds inductively: first translate the smaller fragments that make up a term.

After translating the smaller fragments, connect or wire them together to form the larger fragments and ultimately the complete term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001067","sourceNodeId":"n0001067","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"Every translated fragment has one incoming wire and zero or more outgoing wires; the outer net has one root.","status":"expanded"} -->

Each translated fragment receives exactly one incoming wire, which identifies the fragment that leads into it.

A translated fragment may have no outgoing wires or may have several outgoing wires; the complete outer net has exactly one root.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001070","sourceNodeId":"n0001070","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"An abstraction fan connects its first auxiliary port to the body and its second auxiliary port to the bound variable.","status":"expanded"} -->

An abstraction fan has a first auxiliary port and a second auxiliary port, each used for a distinct connection.

The first auxiliary port connects to the abstraction body, while the second auxiliary port connects to the bound variable.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001071","sourceNodeId":"n0001071","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"An unused bound variable connects the abstraction's variable port to an eraser in ΔA and ΔK.","status":"expanded"} -->

When a bound variable is unused, the abstraction's variable port connects to an eraser.

This eraser connection occurs in both ΔA and ΔK.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001072","sourceNodeId":"n0001072","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"A one-port replicator with zero delta is equivalent to a wire and is omitted from canonical construction.","status":"expanded"} -->

A one-port replicator with zero delta is equivalent to a wire.

**Limitation.** The canonical construction omits a one-port replicator with zero delta because it is equivalent to a wire.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-4fede931d330df5e","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"annihilation","status":"expanded"} -->

Annihilation is a process in which a particle and its corresponding antiparticle meet and convert their combined mass and energy into other particles or forms of energy.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-e4dc860f89307587","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"well-formed lambda translation","status":"expanded"} -->

A well-formed lambda translation represents an expression using the grammar of lambda calculus, with variables, variable-binding abstractions, and function applications arranged in syntactically valid combinations.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001055","sourceNodeId":"n0001055","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"Equal replicators annihilate only when level, arity, and every port delta match; same level suffices for well-formed lambda translations.","status":"expanded"} -->

**Limitation.** Equal replicators annihilate only when their level, arity, and every corresponding port delta all match.

For well-formed lambda translations, matching levels alone is sufficient for the replicators to satisfy the other matching conditions needed for annihilation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-dbc180a389e526e6","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"replica","status":"expanded"} -->

In this Δ-Net reduction, a replica is a copied agent or replicator instance that participates in the reduced network.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001057","sourceNodeId":"n0001057","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"When lower-level R meets higher-level H, R creates one H replica per R auxiliary port.","status":"expanded"} -->

When a lower-level replicator R meets a higher-level replicator H, R creates replicas of H.

R creates exactly one H replica for each auxiliary port of R.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-fc39155b2ba342e3","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"port delta","status":"expanded"} -->

A port delta is the signed integer stored on an auxiliary replicator port, equal to the connected endpoint’s level minus the replicator’s level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001059","sourceNodeId":"n0001059","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"H creates one exact copy of R per H auxiliary port; those R copies preserve R's level and deltas.","status":"expanded"} -->

When the higher-level replicator H meets the lower-level replicator R, H creates one exact R copy for each auxiliary port of H.

Each R copy preserves R's level and all of R's port deltas.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-9d2dcd7d076834d1","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"named boundary node","status":"expanded"} -->

A named boundary node is an explicitly labeled concept that marks the edge of a chapter, topic, or knowledge domain; it identifies where that scope begins or ends.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a23ae1f106d868c8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"rewriting agent","status":"expanded"} -->

A rewriting agent is a software component that modifies existing text according to specified instructions, such as improving clarity, changing style, or preserving required information.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001068","sourceNodeId":"n0001068","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"A free variable becomes a named boundary node rather than a rewriting agent.","status":"expanded"} -->

A free variable is a variable occurrence that is not bound by a binder in its syntactic context.

A free variable becomes a named boundary node.

**Limitation.** A free variable is not a rewriting agent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a4a4126db8fbb58b","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"sharing replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

A sharing replicator is a mechanism that duplicates a shared value or computation so that multiple consumers can use separate copies when sharing the original is no longer appropriate.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001069","sourceNodeId":"n0001069","sourceUnitIds":["u000174","u000175","u000176","u000177","u000178","u000179","u000180","u000181","u000182","u000183","u000184","u000185","u000186","u000187","u000188","u000189","u000190","u000191","u000192","u000193","u000194","u000195","u000196","u000197","u000198","u000199","u000200","u000201","u000202","u000203","u000204","u000205","u000206","u000207","u000208","u000209","u000210","u000211","u000212","u000213","u000214","u000215","u000216","u000217","u000218","u000219","u000220","u000221","u000222","u000223","u000224","u000225","u000226","u000227","u000228","u000229","u000230","u000231","u000232","u000233","u000234","u000235","u000236","u000237","u000238","u000239","u000240","u000241","u000242","u000243","u000244","u000245","u000246","u000247","u000248","u000249"],"sourceAnchor":"A bound variable occurrence is a wire whose endpoint later connects to its owning binder directly or through a sharing replicator.","status":"expanded"} -->

A bound variable occurrence is a use of a variable governed by its owning binder, such as a declaration that introduces the variable.

In a graph representation, the occurrence is modeled as a wire whose endpoint eventually connects to its owning binder either directly or through a sharing replicator.

## 4.3 Replicator meets replicator: equal levels cancel; different levels commute

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000214","sourceNodeId":"n0000214","sourceUnitIds":["u000175"],"sourceAnchor":"### 4.3 Replicator meets replicator: equal levels cancel; different levels commute","status":"source_ready"} -->

Before: equal level = 2

Both replicators are gone.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Same-level annihilation. The lower replicator is rotated, so its clockwise auxiliary-port indices appear right-to-left.

Matching indices reconnect—top d₀ to lower d₀, and so on—making the two outer wires cross. Both replicators are gone.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000226","sourceNodeId":"n0000226","sourceUnitIds":["u000187"],"sourceAnchor":"Two simultaneous outcomes. The upper copies of H inherit R’s three port deltas, so their levels become 7, 10, and 6. The lower copies R¹ and R⁰ leave through H’s ports e₁ and e₀; both are exact copies that keep R’s level 2 and deltas 0, +3, −1. This diagram adapts the structure of [Figure 4 in the source paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4) (CC BY 4.0).","status":"source_ready"} -->

Two simultaneous outcomes. The upper copies of H inherit R’s three port deltas, so their levels become 7, 10, and 6.

The lower copies R¹ and R⁰ leave through H’s ports e₁ and e₀; both are exact copies that keep R’s level 2 and deltas 0, +3, −1. This diagram adapts the structure of [Figure 4 in the source paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S2.F4) (CC BY 4.0).

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000227","sourceNodeId":"n0000227","sourceUnitIds":["u000188"],"sourceAnchor":"Read both halves of the result. Across the top, R’s three auxiliary ports create three H replicas, and each port’s delta shifts that replica’s level. Across the bottom, H’s two auxiliary ports create two exact R copies: R’s level and deltas stay put. The old eⱼ wire stays on the principal port of Rʲ. Between the families, the wires form a complete 3-by-2 grid: auxiliary port j of every H replica meets the matching port of Rʲ.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Read both halves of the result. Across the top, R’s three auxiliary ports create three H replicas, and each port’s delta shifts that replica’s level.

Across the bottom, H’s two auxiliary ports create two exact R copies: R’s level and deltas stay put. The old eⱼ wire stays on the principal port of Rʲ.

Between the families, the wires form a complete 3-by-2 grid: auxiliary port j of every H replica meets the matching port of Rʲ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000228","sourceNodeId":"n0000228","sourceUnitIds":["u000189"],"sourceAnchor":"+","status":"source_ready"} -->

+

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000724","sourceNodeId":"n0000724","sourceUnitIds":["u000176"],"sourceAnchor":"Annihilation removes both replicators and reconnects matching auxiliary ports.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Annihilation removes both replicators and reconnects matching auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000725","sourceNodeId":"n0000725","sourceUnitIds":["u000176"],"sourceAnchor":"Annihilation creates no replicas.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Annihilation creates no replicas.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000726","sourceNodeId":"n0000726","sourceUnitIds":["u000176"],"sourceAnchor":"Hand-built nets require matching arity and every port delta in addition to matching levels.","status":"expanded"} -->

A hand-built net is valid only when the connected levels have matching arity, meaning they expose the same number of ports or connection positions.

Matching levels alone is insufficient: each corresponding port must also have the required port delta, so the net preserves the intended change at every connection.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000727","sourceNodeId":"n0000727","sourceUnitIds":["u000177"],"sourceAnchor":"When levels differ, the replicators commute rather than annihilate.","status":"expanded"} -->

When two replicators operate at different levels, their operations commute: applying one and then the other gives the same result as applying them in the opposite order.

These differently leveled replicators therefore do not annihilate one another; instead, neither operation cancels the other, and their order of application does not affect the outcome.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000728","sourceNodeId":"n0000728","sourceUnitIds":["u000177"],"sourceAnchor":"The lower-level replicator creates one higher-level replica per auxiliary port.","status":"expanded"} -->

The lower-level replicator creates higher-level replicas.

It creates exactly one higher-level replica for each auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000729","sourceNodeId":"n0000729","sourceUnitIds":["u000177"],"sourceAnchor":"The higher-level replicator creates exact lower-level replicas per auxiliary port.","status":"expanded"} -->

The higher-level replicator creates lower-level replicas.

The higher-level replicator’s lower-level replicas are exact and are created per auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000730","sourceNodeId":"n0000730","sourceUnitIds":["u000177"],"sourceAnchor":"Each higher-level replica adds the exit-port delta to its current level.","status":"expanded"} -->

A higher-level replica is a copy of a net component positioned at a higher level in the hierarchy.

Each higher-level replica adds the exit-port delta to its current level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000216","sourceNodeId":"n0000216","sourceUnitIds":["u000177"],"sourceAnchor":"If the levels differ, the replicators are distinct and commute. Call the lower-level one R and the higher-level one H. R makes one replica of H for each of R’s auxiliary ports; H makes exact copies of R for each of H’s ports. Each H replica takes the delta on its exit port through R and adds it to H’s current level. The second diagram shows that different-level case.","status":"source_ready"} -->

If the levels differ, the replicators are distinct and commute. Call the lower-level one R and the higher-level one H.

R makes one replica of H for each of R’s auxiliary ports; H makes exact copies of R for each of H’s ports. Each H replica takes the delta on its exit port through R and adds it to H’s current level.

The second diagram shows that different-level case.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-10a2ff14395b3b33","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"auxiliary output","status":"expanded"} -->

Auxiliary output is additional information produced alongside a main result to provide context, diagnostics, metadata, or supporting details.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-3a689a05fcc9e6b2","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"n-port replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

An n-port replicator is a system with one input and n output ports that copies or distributes the same signal, data, or object to each output.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-72b76468716703f6","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"matching levels","status":"expanded"} -->

Matching levels are corresponding stages, categories, or degrees in two things being compared or aligned.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000723","sourceNodeId":"n0000723","sourceUnitIds":["u000176"],"sourceAnchor":"Matching levels allow annihilation in a well-formed λ translation.","status":"expanded"} -->

The claim concerns a λ translation that is well formed.

Within such a translation, matching levels allow annihilation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000215","sourceNodeId":"n0000215","sourceUnitIds":["u000176"],"sourceAnchor":"First compare the two levels. If they match—and the net came from a well-formed λ translation—the replicators are equal: they annihilate, both nodes disappear, and matching auxiliary ports reconnect. No replicas are created. In a hand-built net, matching levels alone are not enough; arity and every port delta must match too.","status":"source_ready"} -->

First compare the two levels. If they match—and the net came from a well-formed λ translation—the replicators are equal: they annihilate, both nodes disappear, and matching auxiliary ports reconnect.

No replicas are created. In a hand-built net, matching levels alone are not enough; arity and every port delta must match too.

## The operational rule

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000229","sourceNodeId":"n0000229","sourceUnitIds":["u000190"],"sourceAnchor":"### The operational rule","status":"source_ready"} -->

### The operational rule

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000230","sourceNodeId":"n0000230","sourceUnitIds":["u000191"],"sourceAnchor":"Every port carries one integer. When an H replica leaves through that port, add the integer to H’s current level. The delta is the level change stamped on that particular exit.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Every port carries one integer. When an H replica leaves through that port, add the integer to H’s current level. The delta is the level change stamped on that particular exit.

## A worked example

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000231","sourceNodeId":"n0000231","sourceUnitIds":["u000192"],"sourceAnchor":"#### A worked example","status":"source_ready"} -->

#### A worked example

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000232","sourceNodeId":"n0000232","sourceUnitIds":["u000193"],"sourceAnchor":"Suppose R sits at level 2 with two auxiliary ports. The first meets a wire endpoint at level 2, so its delta is 2 − 2 = 0. The second meets an endpoint at level 5, so its delta is 5 − 2 = +3. Now let a higher-level replicator H, at level 7, meet R.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Suppose R sits at level 2 with two auxiliary ports. The first meets a wire endpoint at level 2, so its delta is 2 − 2 = 0.

The second meets an endpoint at level 5, so its delta is 5 − 2 = +3. Now let a higher-level replicator H, at level 7, meet R.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000233","sourceNodeId":"n0000233","sourceUnitIds":["u000194"],"sourceAnchor":"01","status":"source_ready"} -->

01

#### Write the local differences

R’s own level is 2. Each port stores how far its endpoint sits from that 2.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000236","sourceNodeId":"n0000236","sourceUnitIds":["u000197"],"sourceAnchor":"port A: 2 − 2 = 0\nport B: 5 − 2 = +3","status":"source_ready"} -->

port A: 2 − 2 = 0
port B: 5 − 2 = +3

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000237","sourceNodeId":"n0000237","sourceUnitIds":["u000198"],"sourceAnchor":"02","status":"source_ready"} -->

02

#### Copy H once per R port

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000239","sourceNodeId":"n0000239","sourceUnitIds":["u000200"],"sourceAnchor":"H is higher, so R replicates it. Both replicas start from H’s old level: 7.","status":"source_ready"} -->

H is higher, so R replicates it. Both replicas start from H’s old level: 7.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000240","sourceNodeId":"n0000240","sourceUnitIds":["u000201"],"sourceAnchor":"old level of H = 7","status":"source_ready"} -->

old level of H = 7

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000241","sourceNodeId":"n0000241","sourceUnitIds":["u000202"],"sourceAnchor":"03","status":"source_ready"} -->

03

## Add the exit’s delta

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000242","sourceNodeId":"n0000242","sourceUnitIds":["u000203"],"sourceAnchor":"#### Add the exit’s delta","status":"source_ready"} -->

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000243","sourceNodeId":"n0000243","sourceUnitIds":["u000204"],"sourceAnchor":"The copy leaving port A stays at 7. The copy leaving port B shifts up by 3.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The copy leaving port A stays at 7. The copy leaving port B shifts up by 3.

B: 7 + 3 = 10

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000249","sourceNodeId":"n0000249","sourceUnitIds":["u000210"],"sourceAnchor":"Read the calculation from left to right. Port B stores +3. The H replica begins at level 7, then follows that port’s instruction: 7 + 3 = 10.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Read the calculation from left to right. Port B stores +3. The H replica begins at level 7, then follows that port’s instruction: 7 + 3 = 10.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000250","sourceNodeId":"n0000250","sourceUnitIds":["u000211"],"sourceAnchor":"The general rule, after the example","status":"source_ready"} -->

The general rule, after the example
