new level of an H replica = old H level + delta on the R port it leaves through

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000253","sourceNodeId":"n0000253","sourceUnitIds":["u000214"],"sourceAnchor":"How a delta carries context through an interaction","status":"source_ready"} -->

How a delta carries context through an interaction

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000254","sourceNodeId":"n0000254","sourceUnitIds":["u000215"],"sourceAnchor":"Several higher-level replicators can arrive at the same R port with different current levels. The port applies the same local adjustment to each. With delta +3, level 7 becomes 10 and level 12 becomes 15.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Several higher-level replicators can arrive at the same R port with different current levels. The port applies the same local adjustment to each. With delta +3, level 7 becomes 10 and level 12 becomes 15.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000255","sourceNodeId":"n0000255","sourceUnitIds":["u000216"],"sourceAnchor":"Read the pair as an instruction: the replicator level is the current context; the chosen port adds the change for that exit. Chapter 5 shows how translation writes those deltas into the initial net.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Read the pair as an instruction: the replicator level is the current context; the chosen port adds the change for that exit. Chapter 5 shows how translation writes those deltas into the initial net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000256","sourceNodeId":"n0000256","sourceUnitIds":["u000217"],"sourceAnchor":"05 Translation","status":"source_ready"} -->

05 Translation

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000731","sourceNodeId":"n0000731","sourceUnitIds":["u000213"],"sourceAnchor":"Duplicate means an exact copy.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Duplicate means an exact copy.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000732","sourceNodeId":"n0000732","sourceUnitIds":["u000213"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicate allows copies whose levels differ.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Replicate allows copies whose levels differ.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000733","sourceNodeId":"n0000733","sourceUnitIds":["u000213"],"sourceAnchor":"Deltas may be negative, zero, or positive.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Deltas may be negative, zero, or positive.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000252","sourceNodeId":"n0000252","sourceUnitIds":["u000213"],"sourceAnchor":"“Duplicate” means exact copies. “Replicate” is the paper’s word for copies that may differ by level. That distinction does real work: it replaces the separate indexed delimiters of older systems. A delta may be negative, zero, or positive; this example sticks to zero and a positive value only to keep the arithmetic easy to see.","status":"source_ready"} -->

“Duplicate” means exact copies. “Replicate” is the paper’s word for copies that may differ by level.

That distinction does real work: it replaces the separate indexed delimiters of older systems. A delta may be negative, zero, or positive; this example sticks to zero and a positive value only to keep the arithmetic easy to see.

## Compile a λ-term into one rooted net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000257","sourceNodeId":"n0000257","sourceUnitIds":["u000218"],"sourceAnchor":"## Compile a λ-term into one rooted net.","status":"source_ready"} -->

## Compile a λ-term into one rooted net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000259","sourceNodeId":"n0000259","sourceUnitIds":["u000220"],"sourceAnchor":"Vocabulary","status":"source_ready"} -->

Vocabulary

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000260","sourceNodeId":"n0000260","sourceUnitIds":["u000221"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nabs[\"λx.M · parameter x · body M\"]\napp[\"M N · function M · argument N\"]\nocc[\"x₀ and x₁ · two uses of x\"]","status":"source_ready"} -->

Example: (λx. x (g x)) A

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000263","sourceNodeId":"n0000263","sourceUnitIds":["u000224"],"sourceAnchor":"The vocabulary used by the translation. λx.M defines a function; x is its parameter and M is its body. M N applies function M to argument N. In the example, A is the actual argument supplied to the abstraction, while x₀ and x₁ are the two places that use x.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The vocabulary used by the translation. λx.M defines a function; x is its parameter and M is its body. M N applies function M to argument N.

In the example, A is the actual argument supplied to the abstraction, while x₀ and x₁ are the two places that use x.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000735","sourceNodeId":"n0000735","sourceUnitIds":["u000219"],"sourceAnchor":"Each calculus has a bijection from terms to canonical Δ-Nets.","status":"expanded"} -->

A calculus is a formal system whose terms can be translated into Δ-Nets.

The translation is bijective: each term maps to one canonical Δ-Net, and each canonical Δ-Net maps back to one term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000736","sourceNodeId":"n0000736","sourceUnitIds":["u000219"],"sourceAnchor":"Every term maps to one canonical net.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Every term maps to one canonical net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000737","sourceNodeId":"n0000737","sourceUnitIds":["u000219"],"sourceAnchor":"Each canonical net maps back to one term.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Each canonical net maps back to one term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000738","sourceNodeId":"n0000738","sourceUnitIds":["u000219"],"sourceAnchor":"Translation builds a larger net inductively from translated subterms.","status":"expanded"} -->

Translation converts a term into a net representation.

Translation builds a larger net inductively by translating the term's subterms and combining those translated subnets.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000258","sourceNodeId":"n0000258","sourceUnitIds":["u000219"],"sourceAnchor":"For each of λL, λA, λI, and λK, the paper defines a bijection φ from terms to canonical Δ-Nets. A bijection is a one-to-one, reversible map: every term has one canonical net, and that net reads back as one term. The translation is inductive: translate the smaller pieces first, then wire those fragments into the larger term.","status":"source_ready"} -->

For each of λL, λA, λI, and λK, the paper defines a bijection φ from terms to canonical Δ-Nets. A bijection is a one-to-one, reversible map: every term has one canonical net, and that net reads back as one term. The translation is inductive: translate the smaller pieces first, then wire those fragments into the larger term.

## 5.1 Start with a uniform interface

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000264","sourceNodeId":"n0000264","sourceUnitIds":["u000225"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.1 Start with a uniform interface","status":"source_ready"} -->

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000270","sourceNodeId":"n0000270","sourceUnitIds":["u000231"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonical fragment interface. In the abstraction, the parent enters λ’s principal port; λ’s right auxiliary reaches body M, while its left auxiliary reaches the replicator’s principal port when x is shared. In the application, function M reaches the application’s principal port; the parent/result wire and argument N use its two auxiliary ports.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Canonical fragment interface. In the abstraction, the parent enters λ’s principal port; λ’s right auxiliary reaches body M, while its left auxiliary reaches the replicator’s principal port when x is shared. In the application, function M reaches the application’s principal port; the parent/result wire and argument N use its two auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000740","sourceNodeId":"n0000740","sourceUnitIds":["u000226"],"sourceAnchor":"Each fragment has one incoming wire and zero or more outgoing wires.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Each fragment has one incoming wire and zero or more outgoing wires.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000741","sourceNodeId":"n0000741","sourceUnitIds":["u000226"],"sourceAnchor":"The outermost fragment connects to a single root.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The outermost fragment connects to a single root.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-643e5205111b24e4","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"interface node","status":"expanded"} -->

An interface node is a connection point that links one part of a network or structure to another part. It acts as a boundary through which connections or interactions pass.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000742","sourceNodeId":"n0000742","sourceUnitIds":["u000226"],"sourceAnchor":"Free variables become named interface nodes.","status":"expanded"} -->

A free variable is a variable occurrence that is not bound by an abstraction within the term being considered.

Free variables become named interface nodes during translation.

The name on the interface node identifies which free variable the translated net exposes.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-2f716e34e72adabf","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"interface","status":"expanded"} -->

An interface is a defined boundary or set of connection points through which one part interacts with another. It specifies how the parts connect without exposing all of their internal details.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-30b2de9de6f91727","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"fragment interface","status":"expanded"} -->

A fragment interface is the boundary exposed by a fragment, where a fragment is a separately represented piece of a larger structure. It identifies the fragment’s external connection points while leaving its internal structure separate.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000743","sourceNodeId":"n0000743","sourceUnitIds":["u000226"],"sourceAnchor":"The fragment interface allows a translated subterm to plug into its parent.","status":"expanded"} -->

An interface is a set of connection points through which a component connects to surrounding structure.

The fragment interface allows a translated subterm to plug into its parent.

The parent translation uses the fragment's exposed interface to connect that translated subterm into the larger net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000265","sourceNodeId":"n0000265","sourceUnitIds":["u000226"],"sourceAnchor":"Every fragment has an interface: one incoming wire at the top and zero or more outgoing wires at the bottom. The outermost fragment hangs from a single root. Free variables—names no surrounding λ owns—become named interface nodes. That stable shape lets you plug a fragment into its parent the way a function plugs into a call.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Every fragment has an interface: one incoming wire at the top and zero or more outgoing wires at the bottom. The outermost fragment hangs from a single root.

Free variables—names no surrounding λ owns—become named interface nodes. That stable shape lets you plug a fragment into its parent the way a function plugs into a call.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000745","sourceNodeId":"n0000745","sourceUnitIds":["u000243"],"sourceAnchor":"A bound occurrence has no variable agent.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A bound occurrence has no variable agent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000747","sourceNodeId":"n0000747","sourceUnitIds":["u000243"],"sourceAnchor":"Several bound uses connect through a replicator.","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator is a net node that distributes one connection to multiple uses.

Several bound uses connect through a replicator.

The replicator represents the shared connection from the owning abstraction to multiple bound uses.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-b37154d385111e14","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"canonical net interface","status":"expanded"} -->

A net is a set of connected points treated as one connection, and a fragment is a separately represented piece of a larger structure. A canonical net interface is the standardized representation of the boundary through which a fragment connects to that net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000744","sourceNodeId":"n0000744","sourceUnitIds":["u000243"],"sourceAnchor":"A free variable remains a named node at the canonical net interface.","status":"expanded"} -->

A canonical net is the standard net representation associated with a term.

A free variable remains a named node at the canonical net interface.

Thus, the canonical net preserves the free variable's identity at its boundary instead of hiding it as an unnamed internal node.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f710b39ba6b6b399","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"bound use","status":"expanded"} -->

A bound use is an occurrence of a name that has been linked to the particular named definition or object it denotes. The binding identifies which definition or object the occurrence refers to when multiple choices are possible.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-3a72cb76e9b9334c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"abstraction","status":"expanded"} -->

An abstraction is a simplified representation that preserves the details needed for a particular purpose while hiding implementation details. It lets a system be discussed or used at a higher level than its underlying construction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000746","sourceNodeId":"n0000746","sourceUnitIds":["u000243"],"sourceAnchor":"A single bound use connects directly to its owning abstraction.","status":"expanded"} -->

A bound use is an occurrence of a variable governed by an owning abstraction.

A single bound use connects directly to its owning abstraction.

The direct connection is the representation used when the abstraction has exactly one bound use.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000282","sourceNodeId":"n0000282","sourceUnitIds":["u000243"],"sourceAnchor":"The distinction is at the fragment boundary. A free variable survives as a named node in the canonical net’s interface. A bound occurrence introduces no variable agent at all: it is a wire whose endpoint is later connected to its owning λx, directly when there is one use or through Rx when that value is shared. This follows the two variable clauses in [the paper’s inductive translation](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S3.F5).","status":"source_ready"} -->

The distinction is at the fragment boundary. A free variable survives as a named node in the canonical net’s interface.

A bound occurrence introduces no variable agent at all: it is a wire whose endpoint is later connected to its owning λx, directly when there is one use or through Rx when that value is shared.

This follows the two variable clauses in [the paper’s inductive translation](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S3.F5).

## 5.3 The abstraction cases

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000283","sourceNodeId":"n0000283","sourceUnitIds":["u000244"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.3 The abstraction cases","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 5.3 The abstraction cases

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000284","sourceNodeId":"n0000284","sourceUnitIds":["u000245"],"sourceAnchor":"An abstraction is an upward-pointing fan (the λ label is only visual). Its first auxiliary port is the body and its second auxiliary port is the bound variable; in this upward view, those appear on the right and left respectively. If that variable is unused, the second auxiliary port meets an eraser—only in the erasure-capable systems ΔA and ΔK.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An abstraction is an upward-pointing fan (the λ label is only visual). Its first auxiliary port is the body and its second auxiliary port is the bound variable; in this upward view, those appear on the right and left respectively. If that variable is unused, the second auxiliary port meets an eraser—only in the erasure-capable systems ΔA and ΔK.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000286","sourceNodeId":"n0000286","sourceUnitIds":["u000247"],"sourceAnchor":"One bound occurrence · λx.x","status":"source_ready"} -->

One bound occurrence · λx.x

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000748","sourceNodeId":"n0000748","sourceUnitIds":["u000246"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator connects the abstraction’s variable port to multiple concrete use sites.","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator links an abstraction’s variable port to concrete sites where that variable is used.

The same variable port can be linked to multiple concrete use sites rather than only one.

A variable port is an abstraction’s connection point for a variable.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000750","sourceNodeId":"n0000750","sourceUnitIds":["u000246"],"sourceAnchor":"A one-port zero-delta replicator is omitted because it is equivalent to a wire.","status":"expanded"} -->

The object being considered is a one-port zero-delta replicator.

**Limitation.** This replicator is omitted because it is equivalent to a wire.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000752","sourceNodeId":"n0000752","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"The abstraction’s second auxiliary port represents its bound parameter.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The abstraction’s second auxiliary port represents its bound parameter.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000753","sourceNodeId":"n0000753","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"An unused parameter port terminates at an eraser in ΔA and ΔK.","status":"expanded"} -->

In ΔA, an unused parameter port terminates at an eraser.

In ΔK, an unused parameter port terminates at an eraser.

An eraser discards a parameter value rather than passing it to another component.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000755","sourceNodeId":"n0000755","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"Multiple uses connect through a replicator.","status":"expanded"} -->

There are multiple uses.

Multiple uses connect through a replicator.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000756","sourceNodeId":"n0000756","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"The abstraction’s first auxiliary port represents its body.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The abstraction’s first auxiliary port represents its body.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000757","sourceNodeId":"n0000757","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"In λx.x x, the body port connects to the application result and the replicator ports connect to the function and argument.","status":"expanded"} -->

In the expression λx.x x, the lambda abstraction has a body, x x, that applies one occurrence of x to another occurrence of x.

The body port connects to the result of the application, while the replicator's ports connect separately to the function and the argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001073","sourceNodeId":"n0001073","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"An abstraction's bound-variable port connects directly to one occurrence, to an eraser when unused, or to a replicator when several occurrences share one value.","status":"expanded"} -->

An abstraction's bound-variable port is the connection through which the abstraction supplies a value to occurrences of a variable that it binds.

The port connects directly to one occurrence when the bound variable is used once, to an eraser when it is unused, or to a replicator when several occurrences share the same value.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001074","sourceNodeId":"n0001074","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"An application fan connects its principal port to the function, first auxiliary port to the result, and second auxiliary port to the argument.","status":"expanded"} -->

An application fan connects its principal port to the function and its first auxiliary port to the result.

An application fan connects its second auxiliary port to the argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001075","sourceNodeId":"n0001075","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"For an application [M N] at level ℓ, M has level ℓ and N has level ℓ + 1.","status":"expanded"} -->

For an application [M N] at level ℓ, M has level ℓ.

For an application [M N] at level ℓ, N has level ℓ + 1.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001076","sourceNodeId":"n0001076","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"For an abstraction λx.M at level ℓ, its body M remains at level ℓ and a needed sharing replicator Rₓ has level ℓ + 1.","status":"expanded"} -->

For an abstraction λx.M assigned level ℓ, the body M stays at level ℓ rather than moving to a new level.

If the variable x must be shared, its replicator Rₓ is assigned level ℓ + 1, one level above the abstraction's body.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001077","sourceNodeId":"n0001077","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"The whole translated term begins at level 0.","status":"expanded"} -->

The translated term has an initial level assigned to it before any traversal of its syntax begins.

That initial level is 0, which is the outermost level of the whole translated term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001078","sourceNodeId":"n0001078","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"A bound occurrence's wire endpoint has the level reached by walking the source syntax to that occurrence.","status":"expanded"} -->

A bound occurrence is connected to a wire endpoint whose level is determined by traversing the source syntax to that occurrence.

The endpoint therefore receives the level reached at the occurrence's position during that syntax walk.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001079","sourceNodeId":"n0001079","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"Walking a lambda body or an application function preserves the current level; entering an application argument adds one.","status":"expanded"} -->

When traversal enters a lambda body, it keeps the current level unchanged.

When traversal follows an application's function, the level also stays unchanged; entering the application's argument instead increases the level by one.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001080","sourceNodeId":"n0001080","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"A replicator port delta equals its connected occurrence level minus the replicator level.","status":"expanded"} -->

For a replicator port, the connected occurrence has an occurrence level and the replicator has its own replicator level.

**Derivation.** The port delta is computed as the connected occurrence level minus the replicator level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001081","sourceNodeId":"n0001081","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"For λx. x (g x), the two x occurrences have levels 0 and 2, while Rₓ has level 1 and deltas ⟨−1, +1⟩.","status":"expanded"} -->

In λx. x (g x), the first x occurrence has level 0, while the x occurring inside the application argument has level 2.

The sharing replicator Rₓ has level 1, giving its two port deltas as ⟨−1, +1⟩.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001082","sourceNodeId":"n0001082","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"The linear term (λx.x)(λy.y) reduces by one fan annihilation to λy.y.","status":"expanded"} -->

The linear term (λx.x)(λy.y) reduces by one fan annihilation step.

The result of that reduction is λy.y.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001083","sourceNodeId":"n0001083","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"The term λx.x x uses a two-port replicator because one bound input has two consumers.","status":"expanded"} -->

In λx.x x, the bound variable x occurs twice in the body, so its single bound input has two consumers.

Because one input must feed two consumers, the translation uses a two-port replicator.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001084","sourceNodeId":"n0001084","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"For [λx.x x]₀, the replicator has level 1, with function-use delta −1 and argument-use delta 0.","status":"expanded"} -->

For [λx.x x]₀, the replicator has level 1.

For [λx.x x]₀, the function-use delta is −1 and the argument-use delta is 0.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001085","sourceNodeId":"n0001085","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"A level delta is neither a copy count nor a source-level variable value; it records relative sharing context at a consumer.","status":"expanded"} -->

A level delta describes how a consumer shares a level relative to other consumers; it is not a count of copied items.

A level delta also does not store the value of a variable at the source level. Instead, it records the relative sharing context observed at the consumer.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001086","sourceNodeId":"n0001086","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"For λx. x (x (x x)), the binder replicator is at level 1 and its four occurrence deltas are ⟨−1, 0, +1, +2⟩.","status":"expanded"} -->

For the expression λx. x (x (x x)), the binder replicator is at level 1.

The four occurrence deltas for λx. x (x (x x)) are ⟨−1, 0, +1, +2⟩.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001087","sourceNodeId":"n0001087","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"The four occurrence endpoint levels in λx. x (x (x x)) are 0, 1, 2, and 3.","status":"expanded"} -->

The lambda expression λx. x (x (x x)) has four occurrence endpoints.

The four occurrence endpoint levels in this expression are 0, 1, 2, and 3.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001088","sourceNodeId":"n0001088","sourceUnitIds":["u000250","u000251","u000252","u000253","u000254","u000255","u000256","u000257","u000258","u000259","u000260","u000261","u000262","u000263","u000264","u000265","u000266","u000267","u000268","u000269","u000270","u000271","u000272","u000273","u000274","u000275","u000276","u000277","u000278","u000279","u000280","u000281","u000282","u000283","u000284","u000285","u000286","u000287","u000288","u000289","u000290","u000291","u000292","u000293","u000294","u000295","u000296","u000297","u000298","u000299","u000300","u000301","u000302","u000303","u000304","u000305","u000306","u000307","u000308","u000309","u000310","u000311","u000312","u000313","u000314","u000315","u000316","u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"Applying λx. x (x (x x)) to N produces N (N (N N)) by beta-reduction.","status":"expanded"} -->

The term λx. x (x (x x)) is a lambda abstraction whose body applies the bound variable x to the result of applying x to (x x).

**Derivation.** Applying this abstraction to N substitutes N for every occurrence of the bound variable x in its body, yielding N (N (N N)) by beta-reduction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a30f779c977b703e","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"use","status":"expanded"} -->

To use something means to apply it for a particular purpose or task.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000754","sourceNodeId":"n0000754","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"A single use connects directly to the parameter port.","status":"expanded"} -->

There is a single use.

The single use connects directly to the parameter port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000293","sourceNodeId":"n0000293","sourceUnitIds":["u000253"],"sourceAnchor":"Read each picture from its λ binder. The second auxiliary port is the bound parameter x: it closes directly to one occurrence, terminates at E when unused, or reaches R when several occurrences must share one incoming value. The first auxiliary port is the body. In λx.x x that body port meets the application’s result port; the replicator’s auxiliary ports meet the application’s function and argument ports. §5.7 adds the levels and deltas.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Read each picture from its λ binder. The second auxiliary port is the bound parameter x: it closes directly to one occurrence, terminates at E when unused, or reaches R when several occurrences must share one incoming value.

The first auxiliary port is the body. In λx.x x that body port meets the application’s result port; the replicator’s auxiliary ports meet the application’s function and argument ports. §5.7 adds the levels and deltas.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-0db539fe91417b03","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"argument port","status":"expanded"} -->

An argument port is a named interface through which a component, function, or system receives an input argument from another component or caller.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-12ed2850440f0989","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"replicator model","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator model describes how the proportions of competing types change over time: types that perform better than the population average become more common, while types that perform worse become less common. In its standard form, the change in type i's proportion is proportional to its current proportion and its fitness advantage over the average fitness.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f3094460498c2b72","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"replicator auxiliary","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator auxiliary is an auxiliary quantity used alongside a replicator model.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000749","sourceNodeId":"n0000749","sourceUnitIds":["u000246"],"sourceAnchor":"For λx.x x, the two replicator auxiliaries connect to the application’s argument and function ports.","status":"expanded"} -->

In λx.x x, the same bound variable x occurs twice in the term’s body.

The two replicator auxiliaries connect to the application’s argument port and function port, respectively.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000285","sourceNodeId":"n0000285","sourceUnitIds":["u000246"],"sourceAnchor":"When a used variable has several occurrences, a replicator joins the abstraction’s variable port to those uses. Each auxiliary port of R lands on a concrete use site: for λx.x x, one auxiliary meets the application’s argument port and the other meets its function port. The application itself hangs from the abstraction’s body port. A one-port replicator with zero delta is just a wire, so canonical construction leaves it out.","status":"source_ready"} -->

When a used variable has several occurrences, a replicator joins the abstraction’s variable port to those uses. Each auxiliary port of R lands on a concrete use site: for λx.x x, one auxiliary meets the application’s argument port and the other meets its function port.

The application itself hangs from the abstraction’s body port. A one-port replicator with zero delta is just a wire, so canonical construction leaves it out.

## 5.4 The application case

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000294","sourceNodeId":"n0000294","sourceUnitIds":["u000254"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.4 The application case","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 5.4 The application case

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000295","sourceNodeId":"n0000295","sourceUnitIds":["u000255"],"sourceAnchor":"An application is a triangular fan (the app label is only visual). Its principal port is fixed at the top apex and meets the function fragment. Its result and argument auxiliary ports are fixed symmetrically along the bottom edge. During reduction the application fan eventually meets an abstraction fan; their principal-port annihilation is the application.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An application is a triangular fan (the app label is only visual). Its principal port is fixed at the top apex and meets the function fragment.

Its result and argument auxiliary ports are fixed symmetrically along the bottom edge. During reduction the application fan eventually meets an abstraction fan; their principal-port annihilation is the application.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000296","sourceNodeId":"n0000296","sourceUnitIds":["u000256"],"sourceAnchor":"Source syntax","status":"source_ready"} -->

Source syntax

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000297","sourceNodeId":"n0000297","sourceUnitIds":["u000257"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nappS[\"[M N] · ℓ\"]\nMS[\"[M]ℓ · same level\"]\nNS[\"[N]ℓ₊₁ · one deeper\"]\nappS —>|function +0| MS\nappS —>|argument +1| NS","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
appS["[M N] · ℓ"]
MS["[M]ℓ · same level"]
NS["[N]ℓ₊₁ · one deeper"]
appS -->|function +0| MS
appS -->|argument +1| NS

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000298","sourceNodeId":"n0000298","sourceUnitIds":["u000258"],"sourceAnchor":"Three fan ports","status":"source_ready"} -->

Three fan ports

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000299","sourceNodeId":"n0000299","sourceUnitIds":["u000259"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nresN@{ shape: rect, label: \"1st aux · result\" }\nappN@{ shape: triangle, label: \"app\" }\nMf2@{ shape: rect, label: \"function M · ℓ\" }\nNf2@{ shape: rect, label: \"argument N · ℓ+1\" }\nMf2 —>|principal| appN\nappN —- resN\nappN —- Nf2","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
resN@{ shape: rect, label: "1st aux · result" }
appN@{ shape: triangle, label: "app" }
Mf2@{ shape: rect, label: "function M · ℓ" }
Nf2@{ shape: rect, label: "argument N · ℓ+1" }
Mf2 -->|principal| appN
appN --- resN
appN --- Nf2

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000758","sourceNodeId":"n0000758","sourceUnitIds":["u000260"],"sourceAnchor":"The application result uses the first auxiliary port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The application result uses the first auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000759","sourceNodeId":"n0000759","sourceUnitIds":["u000260"],"sourceAnchor":"The argument fragment uses the second auxiliary port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The argument fragment uses the second auxiliary port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000760","sourceNodeId":"n0000760","sourceUnitIds":["u000260"],"sourceAnchor":"The function fragment meets the principal port.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The function fragment meets the principal port.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f9cd3011a4607c23","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"β-redex","status":"expanded"} -->

A β-redex is an expression of the form (λx.M) N in the lambda calculus, where a function that takes x is applied to an argument N and can be simplified by substituting N for x in M.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000761","sourceNodeId":"n0000761","sourceUnitIds":["u000260"],"sourceAnchor":"This wiring lets an abstraction on the function side form a β-redex.","status":"expanded"} -->

A β-redex is an expression where a function abstraction is applied to an argument.

The wiring places an abstraction on the function side of an application.

With the abstraction on the function side, this wiring forms a β-redex.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000300","sourceNodeId":"n0000300","sourceUnitIds":["u000260"],"sourceAnchor":"One syntax constructor, three connections. For [M N]ℓ, the result or parent wire uses the first auxiliary port, [N]ℓ+1 uses the second auxiliary port, and [M]ℓ meets the principal port. This is why a λ-fan connected to the function side can later form the active pair for β-reduction.","status":"source_ready"} -->

One syntax constructor, three connections. For [M N]ℓ, the result or parent wire uses the first auxiliary port, [N]ℓ+1 uses the second auxiliary port, and [M]ℓ meets the principal port. This is why a λ-fan connected to the function side can later form the active pair for β-reduction.

## 5.5 Where levels come from

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000301","sourceNodeId":"n0000301","sourceUnitIds":["u000261"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.5 Where levels come from","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 5.5 Where levels come from

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000302","sourceNodeId":"n0000302","sourceUnitIds":["u000262"],"sourceAnchor":"Keep every word tied to the source syntax. An abstraction is the literal term λx.M: parameter x, body M. An application is the literal term M N: function M, argument N. A bound occurrence of x is one of the x leaves in M owned by that particular λx.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Keep every word tied to the source syntax. An abstraction is the literal term λx.M: parameter x, body M.

An application is the literal term M N: function M, argument N. A bound occurrence of x is one of the x leaves in M owned by that particular λx.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000303","sourceNodeId":"n0000303","sourceUnitIds":["u000263"],"sourceAnchor":"@","status":"source_ready"} -->

@

## “Context” here is not a runtime closure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000304","sourceNodeId":"n0000304","sourceUnitIds":["u000264"],"sourceAnchor":"### “Context” here is not a runtime closure","status":"source_ready"} -->

### “Context” here is not a runtime closure

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000306","sourceNodeId":"n0000306","sourceUnitIds":["u000266"],"sourceAnchor":"λ-calculus syntax | Given | Assign | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

λ-calculus syntax | Given | Assign | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000307","sourceNodeId":"n0000307","sourceUnitIds":["u000267"],"sourceAnchor":"Whole term T | T is the root program. | level(T) = 0 | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Whole term T | T is the root program. | level(T) = 0 | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000308","sourceNodeId":"n0000308","sourceUnitIds":["u000268"],"sourceAnchor":"Abstraction λx.M | level(λx.M) = l | level(M) = l; the replicator Rx belongs to level l + 1. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Abstraction λx.M | level(λx.M) = l | level(M) = l; the replicator Rx belongs to level l + 1. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000309","sourceNodeId":"n0000309","sourceUnitIds":["u000269"],"sourceAnchor":"Application M N | level(M N) = l | level(M) = l; level(N) = l + 1. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Application M N | level(M N) = l | level(M) = l; level(N) = l + 1. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000310","sourceNodeId":"n0000310","sourceUnitIds":["u000270"],"sourceAnchor":"Occurrence x | x is reached at level li. | Its wire endpoint has level li. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Occurrence x | x is reached at level li. | Its wire endpoint has level li. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000311","sourceNodeId":"n0000311","sourceUnitIds":["u000271"],"sourceAnchor":"Use the same instructions at every step. For λx.λy.x, start at level 0 and carry 0 through both bodies, so x sits at level 0. For λx.f (g x), carry 0 into the body, give the outer function f level 0, and give its argument g x level 1. Apply the rule again inside that argument: g stays at 1, and x lands at 2.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Use the same instructions at every step. For λx.λy.x, start at level 0 and carry 0 through both bodies, so x sits at level 0.

For λx.f (g x), carry 0 into the body, give the outer function f level 0, and give its argument g x level 1. Apply the rule again inside that argument: g stays at 1, and x lands at 2.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000312","sourceNodeId":"n0000312","sourceUnitIds":["u000272"],"sourceAnchor":"Walk the syntax tree","status":"source_ready"} -->

Walk the syntax tree

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000313","sourceNodeId":"n0000313","sourceUnitIds":["u000273"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nT[\"[λx. x (g x)]₀\"]\nbinder[\"λx · ℓ = 0\"]\nouter[\"x (g x) · ℓ = 0\"]\nx0[\"x₀ · ℓ = 0\"]\ngx[\"g x · ℓ = 1\"]\ng[\"g · ℓ = 1\"]\nx1[\"x₁ · ℓ = 2\"]\nT —> binder\nbinder —> outer\nouter —>|function +0| x0\nouter —>|argument +1| gx\ngx —>|function +0| g\ngx —>|argument +1| x1","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
T["[λx. x (g x)]₀"]
binder["λx · ℓ = 0"]
outer["x (g x) · ℓ = 0"]
x0["x₀ · ℓ = 0"]
gx["g x · ℓ = 1"]
g["g · ℓ = 1"]
x1["x₁ · ℓ = 2"]
T --> binder
binder --> outer
outer -->|function +0| x0
outer -->|argument +1| gx
gx -->|function +0| g
gx -->|argument +1| x1

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000314","sourceNodeId":"n0000314","sourceUnitIds":["u000274"],"sourceAnchor":"Turn the two x levels into deltas","status":"source_ready"} -->

Turn the two x levels into deltas

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000315","sourceNodeId":"n0000315","sourceUnitIds":["u000275"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nRx@{ shape: trapezoid, label: \"Rₓ · ℓ = 1\" }\np0[\"x₀ · d = 0 − 1 = −1\"]\np1[\"x₁ · d = 2 − 1 = +1\"]\nRx —> p0\nRx —> p1","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
Rx@{ shape: trapezoid, label: "Rₓ · ℓ = 1" }
p0["x₀ · d = 0 − 1 = −1"]
p1["x₁ · d = 2 − 1 = +1"]
Rx --> p0
Rx --> p1

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000317","sourceNodeId":"n0000317","sourceUnitIds":["u000277"],"sourceAnchor":"To encode a term by hand:","status":"source_ready"} -->

To encode a term by hand:

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000318","sourceNodeId":"n0000318","sourceUnitIds":["u000278"],"sourceAnchor":"- Fully parenthesize applications so every function and argument position is unambiguous.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Fully parenthesize applications so every function and argument position is unambiguous.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000319","sourceNodeId":"n0000319","sourceUnitIds":["u000279"],"sourceAnchor":"- Write level 0 on the whole term, then walk inward. Keep the current level through every λx.M body and every function position; add one only when you enter an application’s argument.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Write level 0 on the whole term, then walk inward. Keep the current level through every λx.M body and every function position; add one only when you enter an application’s argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000320","sourceNodeId":"n0000320","sourceUnitIds":["u000280"],"sourceAnchor":"- For each binder λx at level l, put its sharing replicator Rx at level l + 1 when a replicator is needed.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- For each binder λx at level l, put its sharing replicator Rx at level l + 1 when a replicator is needed.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000322","sourceNodeId":"n0000322","sourceUnitIds":["u000282"],"sourceAnchor":"A wire endpoint level is simply the level of that concrete variable occurrence. Its port delta is the gap between where the occurrence sits and where its binder’s replicator sits:","status":"source_ready"} -->

A wire endpoint level is simply the level of that concrete variable occurrence. Its port delta is the gap between where the occurrence sits and where its binder’s replicator sits:

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000323","sourceNodeId":"n0000323","sourceUnitIds":["u000283"],"sourceAnchor":"Construction rule","status":"source_ready"} -->

Construction rule

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000324","sourceNodeId":"n0000324","sourceUnitIds":["u000284"],"sourceAnchor":"port delta = level of its connected wire endpoint − level of the replicator","status":"source_ready"} -->

port delta = level of its connected wire endpoint − level of the replicator

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000325","sourceNodeId":"n0000325","sourceUnitIds":["u000285"],"sourceAnchor":"That is where Chapter 4’s numbers came from: a replicator at level 2 wired to an occurrence at level 5 stores delta +3. During reduction, that +3 is the local adjustment whenever another replicator exits through the port. Section [5.8](#deep-deltas) works the full calculation for four occurrences of one bound variable.","status":"source_ready"} -->

That is where Chapter 4’s numbers came from: a replicator at level 2 wired to an occurrence at level 5 stores delta +3. During reduction, that +3 is the local adjustment whenever another replicator exits through the port. Section [5.8](#deep-deltas) works the full calculation for four occurrences of one bound variable.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000762","sourceNodeId":"n0000762","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"The whole term starts at level 0.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The whole term starts at level 0.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000763","sourceNodeId":"n0000763","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"Abstraction bodies inherit their abstraction’s level.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Abstraction bodies inherit their abstraction’s level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000764","sourceNodeId":"n0000764","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"An application keeps the function at the current level.","status":"source_ready"} -->

An application keeps the function at the current level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000765","sourceNodeId":"n0000765","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"An application assigns the argument level one greater than the current level.","status":"expanded"} -->

When an application assigns an argument, it gives that argument a level equal to the current level plus one.

Thus, if the current level is n, the assigned argument level is n + 1; the assignment always moves one level upward.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000766","sourceNodeId":"n0000766","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"These levels track sharing contexts rather than runtime closure contents.","status":"expanded"} -->

The levels describe which sharing context an argument belongs to.

**Limitation.** These levels do not describe the values or variables captured inside a runtime closure.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000305","sourceNodeId":"n0000305","sourceUnitIds":["u000265"],"sourceAnchor":"Calculate levels by walking the written λ-term. Start the whole term at level 0. For an abstraction λx.M at level ℓ, continue into M at the same level ℓ. For an application M N at level ℓ, keep level ℓ on the function M and write level ℓ + 1 on the argument N. Repeat inside each subtree. During graph reduction those coordinates track sharing contexts; they do not count captured values or surrounding λ symbols the way a runtime closure would.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Calculate levels by walking the written λ-term. Start the whole term at level 0.

For an abstraction λx.M at level ℓ, continue into M at the same level ℓ. For an application M N at level ℓ, keep level ℓ on the function M and write level ℓ + 1 on the argument N.

Repeat inside each subtree. During graph reduction those coordinates track sharing contexts; they do not count captured values or surrounding λ symbols the way a runtime closure would.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000767","sourceNodeId":"n0000767","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"The outer application gives the function occurrence level 0.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The outer application gives the function occurrence level 0.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000768","sourceNodeId":"n0000768","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"The outer application gives its argument subtree level 1.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The outer application gives its argument subtree level 1.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000769","sourceNodeId":"n0000769","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"The inner application keeps g at level 1 and gives its argument x level 2.","status":"expanded"} -->

The inner application keeps g at level 1.

The inner application gives its argument x level 2.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000770","sourceNodeId":"n0000770","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"The binder’s replicator is at level 1.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The binder’s replicator is at level 1.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000771","sourceNodeId":"n0000771","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"The resulting deltas are ⟨−1, +1⟩.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The resulting deltas are ⟨−1, +1⟩.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000316","sourceNodeId":"n0000316","sourceUnitIds":["u000276"],"sourceAnchor":"Walk λx.x (g x) mechanically. Begin at level 0 and keep that level through λx into its body x (g x). At the outer application, give the function x₀ level 0 and the argument g x level 1. Now apply the same rule to g x: g stays at 1 and x₁ lands at 2. The binder’s replicator is at level 1, so the stored deltas are ⟨−1, +1⟩.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Walk λx.x (g x) mechanically. Begin at level 0 and keep that level through λx into its body x (g x).

At the outer application, give the function x₀ level 0 and the argument g x level 1. Now apply the same rule to g x: g stays at 1 and x₁ lands at 2.

The binder’s replicator is at level 1, so the stored deltas are ⟨−1, +1⟩.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000772","sourceNodeId":"n0000772","sourceUnitIds":["u000281"],"sourceAnchor":"Each bound occurrence connects to one auxiliary port of its replicator.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Each bound occurrence connects to one auxiliary port of its replicator.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000773","sourceNodeId":"n0000773","sourceUnitIds":["u000281"],"sourceAnchor":"The port delta equals the occurrence level minus the replicator level.","status":"expanded"} -->

The occurrence level is the level assigned to an occurrence, and the replicator level is the level assigned to its replicator.

The port delta is computed from two levels: the occurrence level and the replicator level.

**Derivation.** Subtract the replicator level from the occurrence level to obtain the port delta: port delta = occurrence level − replicator level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000321","sourceNodeId":"n0000321","sourceUnitIds":["u000281"],"sourceAnchor":"- Connect each bound occurrence xi to one auxiliary port of Rx. If that occurrence was reached at level li, label its port di = li − (l + 1).","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Connect each bound occurrence xi to one auxiliary port of Rx. If that occurrence was reached at level li, label its port di = li − (l + 1).

## 5.6 Follow a β-redex from term, to net, to term

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000326","sourceNodeId":"n0000326","sourceUnitIds":["u000286"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.6 Follow a β-redex from term, to net, to term","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 5.6 Follow a β-redex from term, to net, to term

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000327","sourceNodeId":"n0000327","sourceUnitIds":["u000287"],"sourceAnchor":"The rules feel less abstract when one small program stays on screen the whole way. Start with the closed linear term (λx.x)(λy.y). Each bound variable occurs once, so this is a ΔL example: fans only—no eraser, no replicator.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The rules feel less abstract when one small program stays on screen the whole way. Start with the closed linear term (λx.x)(λy.y). Each bound variable occurs once, so this is a ΔL example: fans only—no eraser, no replicator.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000328","sourceNodeId":"n0000328","sourceUnitIds":["u000288"],"sourceAnchor":"λ-calculus view","status":"source_ready"} -->

λ-calculus view

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000329","sourceNodeId":"n0000329","sourceUnitIds":["u000289"],"sourceAnchor":"(λx.x)(λy.y) →β λy.y","status":"source_ready"} -->

(λx.x)(λy.y) →β λy.y

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000330","sourceNodeId":"n0000330","sourceUnitIds":["u000290"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonical ΔL net — orange link is the active pair","status":"source_ready"} -->

Canonical ΔL net — orange link is the active pair

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000331","sourceNodeId":"n0000331","sourceUnitIds":["u000291"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nrootI((root))\nresI@{ shape: rect, label: \"result\" }\nappI@{ shape: triangle, label: \"app\" }\nargI@{ shape: rect, label: \"argument\" }\nlamx@{ shape: triangle, label: \"λx\" }\nlamy@{ shape: triangle, label: \"λy\" }\nrootI —- resI\nresI —- appI\nappI —>|\"principal ↔ principal\"| lamx\nappI —- argI\nargI —- lamy","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
rootI((root))
resI@{ shape: rect, label: "result" }
appI@{ shape: triangle, label: "app" }
argI@{ shape: rect, label: "argument" }
lamx@{ shape: triangle, label: "λx" }
lamy@{ shape: triangle, label: "λy" }
rootI --- resI
resI --- appI
appI -->|"principal ↔ principal"| lamx

How to read the wiring. The downward @-fan’s first auxiliary port is the application result (the root reaches it); its principal port leads to the function; its second auxiliary port leads to the argument.

The upward λ-fan’s first auxiliary port is its body and its second auxiliary port is its bound variable. Since the body and variable ports of λx.x are already joined, annihilating the two principal ports connects the root directly to λy.y.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000342","sourceNodeId":"n0000342","sourceUnitIds":["u000302"],"sourceAnchor":"What the numbers mean in this exact term. The application hangs from the λ body port: its first auxiliary is the result. One replicator auxiliary meets the application’s argument port; the other meets its function (principal) port. The outer term has level 0. The body application is also level 0; the application rule puts the argument occurrence at level 1, while the function occurrence stays at level 0. The replicator belongs to the surrounding abstraction, so it has level 0 + 1 = 1. Therefore its function-use port stores d0 = 0 − 1 = −1, and its argument-use port stores d1 = 1 − 1 = 0.","status":"source_ready"} -->

What the numbers mean in this exact term. The application hangs from the λ body port: its first auxiliary is the result.

One replicator auxiliary meets the application’s argument port; the other meets its function (principal) port. The outer term has level 0.

The body application is also level 0; the application rule puts the argument occurrence at level 1, while the function occurrence stays at level 0. The replicator belongs to the surrounding abstraction, so it has level 0 + 1 = 1.

Therefore its function-use port stores d0 = 0 − 1 = −1, and its argument-use port stores d1 = 1 − 1 = 0.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000775","sourceNodeId":"n0000775","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"A level delta is not a copy count.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A level delta is not a copy count.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000776","sourceNodeId":"n0000776","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"A level delta is not a source-level variable value.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A level delta is not a source-level variable value.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000777","sourceNodeId":"n0000777","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"A two-port replicator already records two consumers of one bound value.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A two-port replicator already records two consumers of one bound value.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000778","sourceNodeId":"n0000778","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"Levels and deltas record relative sharing contexts at consumer sites.","status":"expanded"} -->

Levels record relative sharing contexts at consumer sites.

Deltas record relative sharing contexts at consumer sites.

A level generally describes the state of something at a particular point or context, rather than a change between states.

A delta generally describes a difference or change between states or contexts.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000779","sourceNodeId":"n0000779","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"Commuting replicators use deltas to adjust replica levels.","status":"expanded"} -->

The replicators described here are commuting replicators.

These commuting replicators use deltas to adjust their replica levels.

A delta is a value that represents a change from one state or quantity to another.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000343","sourceNodeId":"n0000343","sourceUnitIds":["u000303"],"sourceAnchor":"A level delta is not a copy count, and it is not a source-level variable value. The two-port replicator already says “one bound value, two consumers.” Level and per-port deltas only record the relative sharing context at each consumer. When replicators later commute through structure, those deltas adjust the levels of replicas leaving through their ports. That bookkeeping is how Δ-Nets express the sharing a δ would introduce—without separate scope-delimiter agents.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A level delta is not a copy count, and it is not a source-level variable value. The two-port replicator already says “one bound value, two consumers.” Level and per-port deltas only record the relative sharing context at each consumer.

When replicators later commute through structure, those deltas adjust the levels of replicas leaving through their ports. That bookkeeping is how Δ-Nets express the sharing a δ would introduce—without separate scope-delimiter agents.

## 5.8 Let right nesting expose a deeper delta vector

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000344","sourceNodeId":"n0000344","sourceUnitIds":["u000304"],"sourceAnchor":"### 5.8 Let right nesting expose a deeper delta vector","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 5.8 Let right nesting expose a deeper delta vector

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000345","sourceNodeId":"n0000345","sourceUnitIds":["u000305"],"sourceAnchor":"The previous term has only one application, so its two use sites differ by a single level. A clearer closed term is λx. x (x (x x)). The parentheses matter: each successive occurrence on the right sits inside another application’s argument, and every argument edge adds one to the term level.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The previous term has only one application, so its two use sites differ by a single level. A clearer closed term is λx. x (x (x x)).

The parentheses matter: each successive occurrence on the right sits inside another application’s argument, and every argument edge adds one to the term level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000350","sourceNodeId":"n0000350","sourceUnitIds":["u000310"],"sourceAnchor":"Why the vector grows. The outer application hangs from the λ body port. Each inner application hangs from the argument port of the application above it. The four replicator auxiliaries meet the three function ports and the deepest argument port. The abstraction is at level 0, so its replicator is at level 1. The four bound-variable endpoints are at levels 0, 1, 2, 3. Applying di = li − lR yields ⟨−1, 0, +1, +2⟩.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Why the vector grows. The outer application hangs from the λ body port.

Each inner application hangs from the argument port of the application above it. The four replicator auxiliaries meet the three function ports and the deepest argument port.

The abstraction is at level 0, so its replicator is at level 1. The four bound-variable endpoints are at levels 0, 1, 2, 3.

Applying di = li − lR yields ⟨−1, 0, +1, +2⟩.
