<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000356","sourceNodeId":"n0000356","sourceUnitIds":["u000316"],"sourceAnchor":"Apply this abstraction to a term N and the replicator stands for four consumers of that one argument. When later interactions move sharing through the nested applications, exits through the four ports adjust an arriving replica’s level by −1, 0, +1, or +2. The numbers keep track of where each use belongs. They still do not say how many copies to make.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Apply this abstraction to a term N and the replicator stands for four consumers of that one argument. When later interactions move sharing through the nested applications, exits through the four ports adjust an arriving replica’s level by −1, 0, +1, or +2.

The numbers keep track of where each use belongs. They still do not say how many copies to make.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000357","sourceNodeId":"n0000357","sourceUnitIds":["u000317"],"sourceAnchor":"06 Beyond core interaction","status":"source_ready"} -->

06 Beyond core interaction

## Reduction needs housekeeping and a schedule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000359","sourceNodeId":"n0000359","sourceUnitIds":["u000318"],"sourceAnchor":"## Reduction needs housekeeping and a schedule.","status":"source_ready"} -->

## Reduction needs housekeeping and a schedule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000360","sourceNodeId":"n0000360","sourceUnitIds":["u000319"],"sourceAnchor":"The full algorithm is more than the interaction core. It adds canonicalization—cleanup rewrites that restore the special term-like net shape—and, for the nonlinear systems, a global leftmost-outermost schedule.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The full algorithm is more than the interaction core. It adds canonicalization—cleanup rewrites that restore the special term-like net shape—and, for the nonlinear systems, a global leftmost-outermost schedule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001089","sourceNodeId":"n0001089","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Translation produces canonical nets, while proper nets are intermediate nets reachable by zero or more Δ interactions.","status":"expanded"} -->

Translation produces canonical nets.

Proper nets are intermediate nets reachable by zero or more Δ interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001092","sourceNodeId":"n0001092","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"When deleting unreachable material leaves an exposed connection in the net, that connection is terminated by an eraser.","status":"expanded"} -->

Deleting unreachable material from a net can expose a connection that no longer has a valid destination or continuation.

When such an exposed connection appears, an eraser terminates it, removing the now-useless connection from the net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001093","sourceNodeId":"n0001093","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"The erasure sweep may run at the end or earlier to save space; the paper recommends sweeping after applications of abstractions that ignore their arguments when memory matters most.","status":"expanded"} -->

The erasure sweep can be postponed until the end of evaluation or performed earlier during evaluation.

Performing the sweep earlier can reduce memory usage; the paper specifically recommends sweeping after applying abstractions that ignore their arguments when memory pressure is greatest.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001100","sourceNodeId":"n0001100","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"The prescribed nonlinear-system schedule selects an outermost reducible pattern and then the leftmost candidate.","status":"expanded"} -->

The prescribed schedule for simplifying a nonlinear system first chooses a reducible pattern at the outermost applicable level.

If multiple candidate patterns remain at that level, the schedule chooses the leftmost candidate.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001101","sourceNodeId":"n0001101","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"A reducible pair may run early when it will eventually reach leftmost-outermost position unchanged.","status":"expanded"} -->

A reducible pair is a pair of expressions to which a reduction rule can be applied.

Such a pair may be reduced early, before it would normally be selected for evaluation.

**Limitation.** Early reduction is valid only if the pair will eventually be the leftmost outermost reducible expression—the first eligible expression encountered from the left, excluding expressions nested inside another candidate—and will remain unchanged until that point.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001102","sourceNodeId":"n0001102","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"The full algorithm translates a term with φ, reduces through proper nets, normalizes and canonicalizes with Ω, then reads back with φ⁻¹.","status":"expanded"} -->

The full algorithm first translates a term by applying φ, then reduces the resulting structure through proper nets.

After reduction, the algorithm normalizes and canonicalizes the result with Ω, then reads the result back by applying the inverse translation φ⁻¹.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001103","sourceNodeId":"n0001103","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Leftmost-outermost reduction avoids evaluating subnets that will later be erased.","status":"expanded"} -->

Leftmost-outermost reduction chooses the leftmost available outer reduction before reducing inner subnets.

This strategy avoids evaluating a subnet when a surrounding reduction will later erase that subnet, preventing unnecessary computation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001104","sourceNodeId":"n0001104","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicators represent shared use in the graph, avoiding repeated evaluation solely because an expression has multiple consumers.","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator is a graph representation of a shared subexpression or computation that can be referenced by multiple consumers.

Because the graph records the shared use once, the system does not evaluate the same expression repeatedly merely because several consumers depend on it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001105","sourceNodeId":"n0001105","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Perfect confluence permits disjoint active pairs to choose and apply local interaction rules independently.","status":"expanded"} -->

Perfect confluence is a property that makes alternative local reductions compatible, so independently applicable interactions do not conflict.

With perfect confluence, disjoint active pairs can choose and apply their local interaction rules independently.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001107","sourceNodeId":"n0001107","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔA requires erasure canonicalization; ΔI requires replicator merging; ΔK requires erasure, merge, and decay canonicalizations.","status":"expanded"} -->

ΔA requires erasure canonicalization.

ΔI requires replicator merging.

ΔK requires erasure, merge, and decay canonicalizations.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001108","sourceNodeId":"n0001108","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"The three-agent interaction core has the one-step diamond property.","status":"expanded"} -->

The source identifies the interaction core as an interaction among three agents.

The source states that this three-agent interaction core has the one-step diamond property.

The one-step diamond property means that when two one-step interactions start from the same state, each resulting state can take one additional interaction to reach the same state.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-8650ff183d0a118b","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"global schedule","status":"expanded"} -->

A global schedule is a plan that specifies when events, actions, or updates occur across an entire system or process.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-3b9bd475b11ed6e9","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"optimality","status":"expanded"} -->

Optimality is the property of being the best possible choice or outcome according to a defined objective, criterion, or set of constraints.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001109","sourceNodeId":"n0001109","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"For nonlinear systems, the paper claims Church-Rosser confluence and optimality only after adding canonicalization and a global schedule.","status":"expanded"} -->

For nonlinear systems, the paper claims Church-Rosser confluence only after canonicalization and a global schedule are added.

For nonlinear systems, the paper claims optimality only after canonicalization and a global schedule are added.

Church-Rosser confluence means that different valid interaction or rewrite sequences can be continued until they reach a common result.

Canonicalization converts equivalent representations into one chosen canonical form, while a global schedule constrains the interaction order across the whole system.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-e1c021b92c2eb4c2","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"sharing context","status":"expanded"} -->

Sharing context is the surrounding information that multiple operations, components, or processes use together instead of receiving separate copies of it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-473d8b3af3720864","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"variable-arity replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

A variable-arity replicator is a component that copies one input to a configurable number of outputs, where the number of outputs is not fixed in advance.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a28c8fb9a4eeae8d","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"local interactions","status":"expanded"} -->

Local interactions are relationships or exchanges between nearby or directly connected components, where each component responds primarily to information from its immediate neighbors.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-487a289d660fb751","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"global discipline","status":"expanded"} -->

Global discipline is a system-wide rule or constraint that coordinates the behavior of all components, rather than governing only a local interaction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001110","sourceNodeId":"n0001110","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets aims to represent each sharing context with a variable-arity replicator while using local interactions and limited global discipline to avoid preventable work.","status":"expanded"} -->

Δ-Nets aims to represent each sharing context with a variable-arity replicator.

Δ-Nets uses local interactions and limited global discipline to avoid preventable work.

Variable arity means that an operation can handle different numbers of connections or copies instead of one fixed number.

A sharing context is a computation context in which one value, resource, or structure is used by multiple consumers.

Local interactions update directly connected components, whereas global discipline coordinates behavior across a wider system when needed.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-23213449f080a057","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"proper net","status":"expanded"} -->

A proper net is a net that is well-formed under the formalism’s structural rules.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-aeb78774a2d6dbcd","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"back-translation","status":"expanded"} -->

Back-translation is the process of mapping a net representation back into its corresponding term or formal representation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001090","sourceNodeId":"n0001090","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonicalization restores a proper net to the canonical shape expected by back-translation.","status":"expanded"} -->

Canonicalization restores a proper net to a canonical shape.

That canonical shape is the one expected by back-translation.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-7adf009d7aac6962","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"net root","status":"expanded"} -->

The net root is the distinguished root node of a net, serving as the reference point for describing the net’s structure.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-bc9e102f9d40e066","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"node reachability","status":"expanded"} -->

Node reachability is the property of being able to follow edges in a graph from one node to another, possibly through intermediate nodes.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001091","sourceNodeId":"n0001091","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔA and ΔK, erasure canonicalization marks nodes reachable from the root and deletes unmarked material.","status":"expanded"} -->

In ΔA and ΔK, erasure canonicalization marks nodes that are reachable from the root.

After marking the reachable nodes, erasure canonicalization deletes the material that remains unmarked.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-aaf3687984deda93","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"local level test","status":"expanded"} -->

A local level test is a test that checks a property within a specified neighborhood, region, or other limited part of a structure rather than across the entire structure.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001094","sourceNodeId":"n0001094","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔI and ΔK, consecutive replicators can merge when a local level test proves the later replicator is unpaired.","status":"expanded"} -->

In ΔI and ΔK, the relevant objects are consecutive replicators.

The consecutive replicators can merge when a local level test proves that the later replicator is unpaired.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-714c27ce30f8c9a2","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"consecutive replicators","status":"expanded"} -->

Consecutive replicators are replicators that occur one immediately after another in a sequence, with no intervening replicator or other relevant element.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6c652707a80f860b","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"delta d","status":"expanded"} -->

Delta d denotes a change, difference, or increment in the quantity represented by d; its precise meaning depends on the surrounding context.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f7cb961000d37f75","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"level difference","status":"expanded"} -->

A level difference is the amount by which a quantity at one point differs from the quantity at another point. It is calculated by subtracting one level from the other, with the sign indicating which point has the higher level.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001095","sourceNodeId":"n0001095","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"For consecutive replicators A and B joined through delta d, B is known unpaired when 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d.","status":"expanded"} -->

For consecutive replicators A and B joined through delta d, let lA and lB denote their levels.

B is known to be unpaired when its level difference from A satisfies the inequality 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-fee4cf6b04d34843","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"external endpoint level","status":"expanded"} -->

An external endpoint level is the value of a measured or modeled quantity at the boundary point where a system connects to something outside it. This boundary value can serve as the reference for comparing conditions inside the system with conditions beyond it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-9014cef95c55c8e8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"surviving replicator level","status":"expanded"} -->

The surviving replicator level is the degree or stage at which a replicator—an entity capable of making copies of itself—continues to persist and reproduce despite competition, selection, or other pressures.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001096","sourceNodeId":"n0001096","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Replicator merging preserves external endpoint levels while recomputing deltas relative to the surviving replicator's level.","status":"expanded"} -->

When two replicators are merged, the levels of their external endpoints are preserved.

After the merge, deltas are recomputed relative to the level of the replicator that survives.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-cd16ae75c6fd50a8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"replicator port","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator port is an interface or connection point through which a system receives or exposes the information, materials, or signals needed to make a copy of itself or another structure.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-af60024515740198","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"unpaired port","status":"expanded"} -->

An unpaired port is a connection point on a node or component that does not currently have a matching port connected to it.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001097","sourceNodeId":"n0001097","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔK, unpaired replicator ports leading directly to erasers can be removed.","status":"expanded"} -->

In ΔK, unpaired replicator ports can be removed when they lead directly to erasers.

**Limitation.** The removal rule applies only when the replicator port is unpaired and leads directly to an eraser.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-988a1eebc1c47c87","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"one-port unpaired replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

A one-port unpaired replicator is a replicator node with exactly one port, where that port has no matching connection to another node.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c4e086bc0dd58c37","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"delta zero","status":"expanded"} -->

Delta zero means that a quantity or system state has not changed between two compared points in time or two conditions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001098","sourceNodeId":"n0001098","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"A remaining one-port unpaired replicator with delta zero collapses to a wire.","status":"expanded"} -->

A remaining one-port unpaired replicator may have delta zero.

A remaining one-port unpaired replicator with delta zero collapses to a wire.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-58a475ba6ab3c2db","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"schedule","status":"expanded"} -->

A schedule is an organized plan that specifies when tasks, events, or activities are expected to occur.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-ba9aec413f702a90","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"principal-to-principal active pair","status":"expanded"} -->

A principal-to-principal active pair is an interaction in which two authenticated entities, called principals, actively communicate or coordinate directly with each other.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001099","sourceNodeId":"n0001099","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonicalization needs a schedule because it can touch ports that are not principal-to-principal active pairs.","status":"expanded"} -->

Canonicalization needs a schedule.

**Limitation.** Canonicalization can touch ports that are not principal-to-principal active pairs, so it needs a schedule.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6ba0644352d0441f","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"ΔL’s definition of a canonical net","status":"expanded"} -->

A canonical net is a designated standard representation for describing equivalent network structures.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-45fba7301e90e79c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"Meaning of beta-reduction","status":"expanded"} -->

Beta-reduction is the process of simplifying a function application by replacing the function's parameter with the supplied argument in the function body. For example, applying \(\lambda x.x+1\) to \(3\) beta-reduces to \(3+1\), which evaluates to \(4\).

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d461dd3e578d2697","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"Meaning of interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

An interaction is a way that two or more things affect one another, so the outcome depends on their relationship rather than on each thing acting independently.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a07a900b8341f053","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"Meaning of interaction order","status":"expanded"} -->

Interaction order is the number of entities or variables that a modeled term jointly depends on. An order-1 term is a single-entity or main-effect term, while interactions conventionally begin at order 2 and involve pairs or larger groups.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-f2c70ca67a0eb742","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"canonical net","status":"expanded"} -->

A canonical net is a standardized network representation that captures the essential structure of a system while omitting irrelevant variation, making the system easier to describe, compare, or analyze.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-203f7333b43c38be","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"core Δ-interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

“Core Δ-interaction” is the name of a central interaction in the model; the supplied context does not specify what the interaction consists of or what Δ means.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-d7afa6df218746e1","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"ΔL’s definition of a proper net","status":"expanded"} -->

A proper ΔL net is any net reachable from a canonical net by applying zero or more core Δ-interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0001106","sourceNodeId":"n0001106","sourceUnitIds":["u000318","u000319","u000320","u000321","u000322","u000323","u000324","u000325","u000326","u000327","u000328","u000329","u000330","u000331","u000332","u000333","u000334","u000335","u000336","u000337","u000338","u000339","u000340","u000341","u000342","u000343","u000344","u000345","u000346","u000347","u000348","u000349","u000350","u000351","u000352","u000353","u000354","u000355","u000356","u000357","u000358","u000359","u000360","u000361","u000362","u000363","u000364","u000365","u000366","u000367","u000368","u000369","u000370","u000371","u000372","u000373","u000374","u000375","u000376","u000377","u000378","u000379","u000380","u000381","u000382","u000383","u000384","u000385","u000386","u000387","u000388","u000389","u000390","u000391","u000392","u000393","u000394","u000395","u000396","u000397"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔL, every proper net is canonical and n beta-reductions correspond to n interactions in any order.","status":"expanded"} -->

In ΔL, every proper net is canonical.

In ΔL, n beta-reductions correspond to n interactions, and this correspondence holds regardless of the order in which the interactions occur.

## 6.1 Canonical and proper are different states

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000361","sourceNodeId":"n0000361","sourceUnitIds":["u000320"],"sourceAnchor":"### 6.1 Canonical and proper are different states","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 6.1 Canonical and proper are different states

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000362","sourceNodeId":"n0000362","sourceUnitIds":["u000321"],"sourceAnchor":"Translation produces a canonical net: the standard shape the reverse translation knows how to read. After zero or more core interactions you have a proper net—any intermediate shape reachable during reduction. Canonicalization is the cleanup that walks a proper net back to canonical form.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Translation produces a canonical net: the standard shape the reverse translation knows how to read. After zero or more core interactions you have a proper net—any intermediate shape reachable during reduction. Canonicalization is the cleanup that walks a proper net back to canonical form.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000363","sourceNodeId":"n0000363","sourceUnitIds":["u000322"],"sourceAnchor":"State | Meaning in the paper | Why a reader should care | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

State | Meaning in the paper | Why a reader should care | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000364","sourceNodeId":"n0000364","sourceUnitIds":["u000323"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonical | Directly produced by the φ translation and in the special normal shape the back-translation expects. | It is the stable, term-like representation. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Canonical | Directly produced by the φ translation and in the special normal shape the back-translation expects. | It is the stable, term-like representation. | |

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000365","sourceNodeId":"n0000365","sourceUnitIds":["u000324"],"sourceAnchor":"Proper | Reachable from a canonical net by zero or more Δ interactions. | It is the broader space where reduction does its work. | |","status":"source_ready"} -->

Proper | Reachable from a canonical net by zero or more Δ interactions. | It is the broader space where reduction does its work. | |

## 6.2 Erasure needs a root-reachability sweep

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000366","sourceNodeId":"n0000366","sourceUnitIds":["u000325"],"sourceAnchor":"### 6.2 Erasure needs a root-reachability sweep","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 6.2 Erasure needs a root-reachability sweep

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000367","sourceNodeId":"n0000367","sourceUnitIds":["u000326"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔA and ΔK, an eraser can leave disconnected subnets—smaller graph regions—behind. Erasure canonicalization gathers that unreachable material in one reachability sweep:","status":"source_ready"} -->

In ΔA and ΔK, an eraser can leave disconnected subnets—smaller graph regions—behind. Erasure canonicalization gathers that unreachable material in one reachability sweep:

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000368","sourceNodeId":"n0000368","sourceUnitIds":["u000327"],"sourceAnchor":"A","status":"source_ready"} -->

A

## Mark from the root

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000369","sourceNodeId":"n0000369","sourceUnitIds":["u000328"],"sourceAnchor":"### Mark from the root","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Mark from the root

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000370","sourceNodeId":"n0000370","sourceUnitIds":["u000329"],"sourceAnchor":"Walk parent–child wires from the root and mark every node you reach.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Walk parent–child wires from the root and mark every node you reach.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000371","sourceNodeId":"n0000371","sourceUnitIds":["u000330"],"sourceAnchor":"B","status":"source_ready"} -->

B

## Discard what was not marked

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000372","sourceNodeId":"n0000372","sourceUnitIds":["u000331"],"sourceAnchor":"### Discard what was not marked","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Discard what was not marked

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000373","sourceNodeId":"n0000373","sourceUnitIds":["u000332"],"sourceAnchor":"Delete the unmarked nodes. Wires that still touch them end in erasers instead.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Delete the unmarked nodes. Wires that still touch them end in erasers instead.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000374","sourceNodeId":"n0000374","sourceUnitIds":["u000333"],"sourceAnchor":"C","status":"source_ready"} -->

C

## Choose the space/time trade-off

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000375","sourceNodeId":"n0000375","sourceUnitIds":["u000334"],"sourceAnchor":"### Choose the space/time trade-off","status":"source_ready"} -->

### Choose the space/time trade-off

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000376","sourceNodeId":"n0000376","sourceUnitIds":["u000335"],"sourceAnchor":"Run the sweep at the end, or earlier to save space. When memory matters most, the paper recommends it after every application of an abstraction that ignores its argument.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Run the sweep at the end, or earlier to save space. When memory matters most, the paper recommends it after every application of an abstraction that ignores its argument.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000377","sourceNodeId":"n0000377","sourceUnitIds":["u000336"],"sourceAnchor":"1 · Proper net with a detached island","status":"source_ready"} -->

1 · Proper net with a detached island

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000378","sourceNodeId":"n0000378","sourceUnitIds":["u000337"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nrootS((root))\nkeep[\"reachable subnet\"]\nisland[\"unmarked island\"]\nrootS —> keep","status":"source_ready"} -->

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000379","sourceNodeId":"n0000379","sourceUnitIds":["u000338"],"sourceAnchor":"2–3 · Mark, then delete unmarked","status":"source_ready"} -->

2–3 · Mark, then delete unmarked

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000380","sourceNodeId":"n0000380","sourceUnitIds":["u000339"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart TB\nrootC((root))\nkeep2[\"reachable subnet\"]\nEcap[\"E · exposed wires\"]\nrootC —> keep2\nkeep2 -.-> Ecap","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart TB
rootC((root))

keep2 -.-> Ecap

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000781","sourceNodeId":"n0000781","sourceUnitIds":["u000340"],"sourceAnchor":"Erasure canonicalization is a global cleanup pass rather than a principal-port interaction.","status":"expanded"} -->

Erasure canonicalization is performed as a global cleanup pass.

**Limitation.** It is not performed as an interaction between principal ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000782","sourceNodeId":"n0000782","sourceUnitIds":["u000340"],"sourceAnchor":"The sweep starts at the distinguished root and marks reachable material.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The sweep starts at the distinguished root and marks reachable material.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000783","sourceNodeId":"n0000783","sourceUnitIds":["u000340"],"sourceAnchor":"Unmarked graph material is deleted.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Unmarked graph material is deleted.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000784","sourceNodeId":"n0000784","sourceUnitIds":["u000340"],"sourceAnchor":"Deleting an unmarked node can expose a surviving connection that must be terminated by an eraser.","status":"expanded"} -->

Deleting a node that has no mark can reveal a connection that remains in the graph.

That surviving connection must then be terminated by an eraser.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000381","sourceNodeId":"n0000381","sourceUnitIds":["u000340"],"sourceAnchor":"This is a global cleanup pass, not a principal-port interaction. The sweep begins at the distinguished root, marks the graph material reachable through the parent–child wiring, and deletes everything else. Where deleting an unmarked node exposes a connection that remains in the net, that connection is terminated by E. The schematic follows the erasure canonicalization described in [Section 4 of the paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S4).","status":"source_ready"} -->

This is a global cleanup pass, not a principal-port interaction. The sweep begins at the distinguished root, marks the graph material reachable through the parent–child wiring, and deletes everything else.

Where deleting an unmarked node exposes a connection that remains in the net, that connection is terminated by E. The schematic follows the erasure canonicalization described in [Section 4 of the paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S4).

## 6.3 Sharing needs replicator cleanup

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000382","sourceNodeId":"n0000382","sourceUnitIds":["u000341"],"sourceAnchor":"### 6.3 Sharing needs replicator cleanup","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 6.3 Sharing needs replicator cleanup

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000383","sourceNodeId":"n0000383","sourceUnitIds":["u000342"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔI and ΔK, interactions can build trees made only of replicators. A merge rule collapses consecutive replicators once a local level test shows they are unpaired. Pairing information stays intact; the tree gets shorter.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In ΔI and ΔK, interactions can build trees made only of replicators. A merge rule collapses consecutive replicators once a local level test shows they are unpaired. Pairing information stays intact; the tree gets shorter.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000384","sourceNodeId":"n0000384","sourceUnitIds":["u000343"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000391","sourceNodeId":"n0000391","sourceUnitIds":["u000350"],"sourceAnchor":"The inequality establishes safety; endpoint levels determine the new numbers. Here A is known unpaired, and its connecting port has d = 3. Since 0 ≤ lB − lA = 2 ≤ 3, B is also known unpaired and the consecutive agents may merge. The surviving replicator keeps level 2; the external endpoint levels remain 2, 3, 6, so the new deltas are ⟨0, +1, +4⟩.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The inequality establishes safety; endpoint levels determine the new numbers. Here A is known unpaired, and its connecting port has d = 3.

Since 0 ≤ lB − lA = 2 ≤ 3, B is also known unpaired and the consecutive agents may merge. The surviving replicator keeps level 2; the external endpoint levels remain 2, 3, 6, so the new deltas are ⟨0, +1, +4⟩.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000397","sourceNodeId":"n0000397","sourceUnitIds":["u000356"],"sourceAnchor":"Decay removes only eraser-bound auxiliary ports. In this ΔK example, the two E-bound exits contribute no surviving consumers, so they are deleted. The remaining one-port replicator has delta 0; by the canonical representation rule it carries no information beyond the connection itself and collapses to a wire. The paper applies this cleanup during erasure canonicalization or lazily just before relevant replication rules.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Decay removes only eraser-bound auxiliary ports. In this ΔK example, the two E-bound exits contribute no surviving consumers, so they are deleted.

The remaining one-port replicator has delta 0; by the canonical representation rule it carries no information beyond the connection itself and collapses to a wire. The paper applies this cleanup during erasure canonicalization or lazily just before relevant replication rules.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000787","sourceNodeId":"n0000787","sourceUnitIds":["u000345"],"sourceAnchor":"The proof says B cannot interact before A is annihilated.","status":"expanded"} -->

The proof says that B cannot interact before A is annihilated.

The proof imposes an order: A’s annihilation must occur before any interaction involving B that the proof considers.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000788","sourceNodeId":"n0000788","sourceUnitIds":["u000345"],"sourceAnchor":"The inequality is a safety proof rather than a performance heuristic.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The inequality is a safety proof rather than a performance heuristic.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000791","sourceNodeId":"n0000791","sourceUnitIds":["u000351"],"sourceAnchor":"A remaining one-port zero-delta replicator collapses to a wire.","status":"expanded"} -->

A wire is a direct connection that carries structure or signals without applying a transformation.

A remaining one-port replicator with zero delta collapses to a wire.

After this collapse, the remaining replicator is represented as a direct wire connection.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-133db277ebb46b0f","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"definition of consecutive replicators","status":"expanded"} -->

Two replicators are consecutive when a port of one is directly connected to a port of the other.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-6296d5b32c1729c5","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"definition of replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

A replicator is an entity or process that produces copies of itself, using information or instructions it contains or carries.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-e1881a6d333a0a72","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"meaning of unpaired","status":"expanded"} -->

Unpaired means that an item, person, or component does not have a corresponding partner or match in the relevant set.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000786","sourceNodeId":"n0000786","sourceUnitIds":["u000345"],"sourceAnchor":"The condition proves that B is unpaired when A is known unpaired.","status":"expanded"} -->

The implication assumes that A is known to be unpaired.

Given that assumption, the condition 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d proves that B is unpaired.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-9917dcbb565ee488","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"unpaired replicator","status":"expanded"} -->

An unpaired replicator is a replicating system whose copies are produced without forming a complementary partner or matched pair during replication.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000790","sourceNodeId":"n0000790","sourceUnitIds":["u000351"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔK removes auxiliary ports of unpaired replicators that lead directly to erasers.","status":"expanded"} -->

ΔK is an operation on unpaired replicators that removes auxiliary ports when those ports lead directly to erasers.

For an unpaired replicator whose auxiliary ports lead directly to erasers, ΔK removes those auxiliary ports.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000392","sourceNodeId":"n0000392","sourceUnitIds":["u000351"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔK has one more cleanup: unpaired replicator decay. Drop auxiliary ports of unpaired replicators that lead straight into erasers. If one zero-delta port remains, the replicator collapses to the wire it already was.","status":"source_ready"} -->

ΔK has one more cleanup: unpaired replicator decay. Drop auxiliary ports of unpaired replicators that lead straight into erasers. If one zero-delta port remains, the replicator collapses to the wire it already was.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-3c54b7aad002dd7f","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"source-specific definitions of l_A, l_B, and d","status":"expanded"} -->

In the unpaired-replicator criterion, l_A and l_B are the levels of consecutive replicators A and B, and d is the delta on the port joining them. The condition from the source is zero less than or equal to l_B minus l_A less than or equal to d.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-8fa2f2c64c39ad2d","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"meaning of lA, lB, and d","status":"expanded"} -->

**Prerequisite.** Understanding this notation requires the source-specific definitions of l_A, l_B, and d; their meanings are unresolved in the available context.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000785","sourceNodeId":"n0000785","sourceUnitIds":["u000345"],"sourceAnchor":"For consecutive replicators A and B, the condition is 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d.","status":"expanded"} -->

For consecutive replicators A and B, the difference lB − lA is nonnegative, expressed as 0 ≤ lB − lA.

For consecutive replicators A and B, the difference lB − lA is no greater than d, expressed as lB − lA ≤ d.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000386","sourceNodeId":"n0000386","sourceUnitIds":["u000345"],"sourceAnchor":"For consecutive replicators A and B joined through a port with delta d, B is known unpaired when 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d. In the paper’s reading, B cannot interact before A is annihilated—and since A is unpaired, that never happens. The inequality is a safety proof, not a performance hint.","status":"source_ready"} -->

For consecutive replicators A and B joined through a port with delta d, B is known unpaired when 0 ≤ lB − lA ≤ d. In the paper’s reading, B cannot interact before A is annihilated—and since A is unpaired, that never happens. The inequality is a safety proof, not a performance hint.

## 6.4 Why the algorithm specifies leftmost-outermost

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000398","sourceNodeId":"n0000398","sourceUnitIds":["u000357"],"sourceAnchor":"### 6.4 Why the algorithm specifies leftmost-outermost","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 6.4 Why the algorithm specifies leftmost-outermost

Choose work from the outside, then leftward

Important nuance. The paper also permits a reducible pair to run early if it will eventually reach the leftmost-outermost position unchanged. This preserves some safe parallelism while retaining the schedule’s optimality rationale.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000403","sourceNodeId":"n0000403","sourceUnitIds":["u000362"],"sourceAnchor":"07 Synthesis","status":"source_ready"} -->

07 Synthesis

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000793","sourceNodeId":"n0000793","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"Core interaction rules are locally confluent.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Core interaction rules are locally confluent.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000794","sourceNodeId":"n0000794","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"Canonicalization can modify ports outside principal-to-principal pairs.","status":"expanded"} -->

Canonicalization normalizes equivalent representations into a standard form.

Canonicalization is not limited to principal-to-principal port pairs.

This ability includes modifying ports outside the principal-to-principal pairs.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000795","sourceNodeId":"n0000795","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"A schedule is therefore required for canonicalization.","status":"source_ready"} -->

A schedule is therefore required for canonicalization.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-aee61500ae5d300c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"reducible pattern","status":"expanded"} -->

A reducible pattern is a recurring structure or behavior that can be transformed into a simpler, equivalent form without changing the result that matters.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-74909210b58928ba","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"outermost level","status":"expanded"} -->

The outermost level is the highest or most externally positioned layer in a nested structure, such as the boundary of a set of concentric layers.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-5ff53015f87f2981","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"leftmost candidate","status":"expanded"} -->

The leftmost candidate is the candidate positioned farthest to the left in an ordered list, sequence, diagram, or set of alternatives.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000796","sourceNodeId":"n0000796","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"Leftmost-outermost chooses an outermost reducible pattern and then the leftmost candidate.","status":"expanded"} -->

A reducible pattern is a pattern to which an available reduction rule can be applied.

Leftmost-outermost chooses an outermost reducible pattern.

When there is more than one outermost reducible pattern, leftmost-outermost chooses the leftmost candidate.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-21eed55e04913a7c","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"cleanup","status":"expanded"} -->

Cleanup is the process of removing obsolete, redundant, or temporary material so that the remaining content is consistent and easier to use.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-cb852f3b231d9201","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"merge","status":"expanded"} -->

A merge is the process of combining separate pieces of content, data, or structure into one integrated result while preserving the information that should remain.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000797","sourceNodeId":"n0000797","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"The schedule is used for ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK so cleanup runs early and merges precede later commutations.","status":"expanded"} -->

A schedule specifies the order in which operations are performed.

The schedule is used for each of ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK.

Within that schedule, cleanup runs early, and merges occur before later commutations.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000399","sourceNodeId":"n0000399","sourceUnitIds":["u000358"],"sourceAnchor":"Core interaction rules enjoy local confluence. Canonicalization can touch ports that are not a principal-to-principal pair, so it needs a schedule. Leftmost-outermost first picks an outermost reducible pattern—one with no reducible pattern wrapped around it—then takes the leftmost candidate in the term-like reading of the net. “Sequential” here just means that priority rule chooses the next interaction. The paper uses that order for ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK so cleanup runs early and merges beat later commutations.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Core interaction rules enjoy local confluence. Canonicalization can touch ports that are not a principal-to-principal pair, so it needs a schedule.

Leftmost-outermost first picks an outermost reducible pattern—one with no reducible pattern wrapped around it—then takes the leftmost candidate in the term-like reading of the net. “Sequential” here just means that priority rule chooses the next interaction.

The paper uses that order for ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK so cleanup runs early and merges beat later commutations.

## What does this proposal actually promise?

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000404","sourceNodeId":"n0000404","sourceUnitIds":["u000363"],"sourceAnchor":"## What does this proposal actually promise?","status":"source_ready"} -->

## What does this proposal actually promise?

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000405","sourceNodeId":"n0000405","sourceUnitIds":["u000364"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper’s final claim is an algorithm for optimal parallel λ-reduction: translate a term to a canonical net, reduce with the core rules plus the required cleanups and schedule, then read the canonical net back as a term.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The paper’s final claim is an algorithm for optimal parallel λ-reduction: translate a term to a canonical net, reduce with the core rules plus the required cleanups and schedule, then read the canonical net back as a term.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000406","sourceNodeId":"n0000406","sourceUnitIds":["u000365"],"sourceAnchor":"The whole algorithm in one line","status":"source_ready"} -->

The whole algorithm in one line

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000407","sourceNodeId":"n0000407","sourceUnitIds":["u000366"],"sourceAnchor":"flowchart LR\nt[\"t · source λ-term\"]\nphi[\"φ(t) · canonical net\"]\nproper[\"Δ* · proper states\"]\nomega[\"Ω(φ(t)) · normal canonical\"]\ntp[\"t′ · λ normal form\"]\nt —>|encode| phi\nphi —>|interact| proper\nproper —>|normalize + canonicalize| omega\nomega —>|φ⁻¹ read back| tp","status":"source_ready"} -->

flowchart LR
t["t · source λ-term"]
phi["φ(t) · canonical net"]
proper["Δ* · proper states"]
omega["Ω(φ(t)) · normal canonical"]
tp["t′ · λ normal form"]
t -->|encode| phi
phi -->|interact| proper
proper -->|normalize + canonicalize| omega
omega -->|φ⁻¹ read back| tp

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000409","sourceNodeId":"n0000409","sourceUnitIds":["u000368"],"sourceAnchor":"01 / NO DISCARDED WORK","status":"source_ready"} -->

01 / NO DISCARDED WORK

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000798","sourceNodeId":"n0000798","sourceUnitIds":["u000367"],"sourceAnchor":"The bijection φ creates the initial canonical net.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The bijection φ creates the initial canonical net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000799","sourceNodeId":"n0000799","sourceUnitIds":["u000367"],"sourceAnchor":"Core interactions may move the net into the broader space of proper nets.","status":"expanded"} -->

Core interactions may move the net into the broader space of proper nets.

The space of proper nets is the broader space into which core interactions may move the net.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000800","sourceNodeId":"n0000800","sourceUnitIds":["u000367"],"sourceAnchor":"The subsystem’s interaction order and canonicalizations are collected by Ω.","status":"expanded"} -->

Ω collects the subsystem’s interaction order.

Ω collects the subsystem’s canonicalizations.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-64411cfae0a374e3","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"normal","status":"expanded"} -->

Normal means conforming to an expected, usual, or standard condition rather than being unusual or exceptional.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-a7ac2cd2dfd0cdc8","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"canonical","status":"expanded"} -->

Canonical means the standard, agreed reference form or version used to identify or compare something.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000801","sourceNodeId":"n0000801","sourceUnitIds":["u000367"],"sourceAnchor":"The net is read back by φ−1 only after it is normal and canonical.","status":"expanded"} -->

**Prerequisite.** Before readback is allowed, the net must satisfy a joint condition: it is normal and canonical.

Once that joint condition holds, the net is read back through the inverse mapping φ−1.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000408","sourceNodeId":"n0000408","sourceUnitIds":["u000367"],"sourceAnchor":"Keep the state changes separate. The bijection φ creates the initial canonical net. Core interactions enter the broader space of proper nets; the subsystem’s prescribed interaction order and canonicalizations are collected by Ω until the net is both normal and canonical. Only then does φ−1 read it back as a λ-term. This is the construction formalized near the end of [Section 4 of the paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S4).","status":"source_ready"} -->

Keep the state changes separate. The bijection φ creates the initial canonical net.

Core interactions enter the broader space of proper nets; the subsystem’s prescribed interaction order and canonicalizations are collected by Ω until the net is both normal and canonical. Only then does φ−1 read it back as a λ-term.

This is the construction formalized near the end of [Section 4 of the paper](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.20314v4#S4).

ΔL has fan annihilation as its only interaction.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000804","sourceNodeId":"n0000804","sourceUnitIds":["u000378"],"sourceAnchor":"Every proper ΔL net is already canonical.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Every proper ΔL net is already canonical.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000805","sourceNodeId":"n0000805","sourceUnitIds":["u000378"],"sourceAnchor":"If a λL term normalizes in n β-reductions, its ΔL encoding normalizes in n interactions regardless of order.","status":"expanded"} -->

If a λL term normalizes in n β-reductions, then its ΔL encoding normalizes in n interactions as well.

**Limitation.** The ΔL encoding reaches normalization in n interactions regardless of the order of those interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000806","sourceNodeId":"n0000806","sourceUnitIds":["u000378"],"sourceAnchor":"ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK add canonicalization for erasure and sharing structure.","status":"expanded"} -->

Canonicalization means putting a representation into a consistent standard form.

ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK add canonicalization.

The canonicalization applies to erasure and sharing structure.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000419","sourceNodeId":"n0000419","sourceUnitIds":["u000378"],"sourceAnchor":"In ΔL, fan annihilation is the only interaction. The paper states that every proper net is already canonical, and that if n β-reductions normalize a λL term, n interactions normalize its ΔL encoding in any order. ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK add canonicalization for erasure and sharing structure.","status":"source_ready"} -->

In ΔL, fan annihilation is the only interaction. The paper states that every proper net is already canonical, and that if n β-reductions normalize a λL term, n interactions normalize its ΔL encoding in any order. ΔA, ΔI, and ΔK add canonicalization for erasure and sharing structure.

## 7.2 A useful way to separate the claims

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000425","sourceNodeId":"n0000425","sourceUnitIds":["u000384"],"sourceAnchor":"### 7.2 A useful way to separate the claims","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 7.2 A useful way to separate the claims

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000426","sourceNodeId":"n0000426","sourceUnitIds":["u000385"],"sourceAnchor":"- Expressiveness: every term in the four λ fragments has a canonical net, via a stated bijection.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Expressiveness: every term in the four λ fragments has a canonical net, via a stated bijection.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000427","sourceNodeId":"n0000427","sourceUnitIds":["u000386"],"sourceAnchor":"- Local behavior: the three-agent core is an interaction system with the one-step diamond property.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Local behavior: the three-agent core is an interaction system with the one-step diamond property.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000429","sourceNodeId":"n0000429","sourceUnitIds":["u000388"],"sourceAnchor":"The split matters. The third layer needs erasure sweeps, replicator merging, decay, and leftmost-outermost scheduling on top of the core rules.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The split matters. The third layer needs erasure sweeps, replicator merging, decay, and leftmost-outermost scheduling on top of the core rules.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000807","sourceNodeId":"n0000807","sourceUnitIds":["u000387"],"sourceAnchor":"The nonlinear systems require canonicalization and a global schedule for the whole-algorithm claim.","status":"expanded"} -->

A global schedule is a plan that coordinates execution across an entire algorithm.

**Prerequisite.** The nonlinear systems require canonicalization.

The nonlinear systems require a global schedule for the whole-algorithm claim.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000808","sourceNodeId":"n0000808","sourceUnitIds":["u000387"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper claims Church–Rosser confluence and optimality for those systems.","status":"expanded"} -->

The paper claims that the systems under discussion have Church–Rosser confluence.

The paper also claims that these systems are optimal according to the relevant notion of optimality.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000809","sourceNodeId":"n0000809","sourceUnitIds":["u000387"],"sourceAnchor":"Church–Rosser means divergent paths can be continued to a common result.","status":"expanded"} -->

Church–Rosser confluence means that when a system has multiple divergent paths from the same starting point, those paths can each be continued until they reach a common result.

Thus, different intermediate choices do not prevent the system from eventually agreeing on the same outcome.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000810","sourceNodeId":"n0000810","sourceUnitIds":["u000387"],"sourceAnchor":"Church–Rosser does not require the paths to agree after one step.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Church–Rosser does not require the paths to agree after one step.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000428","sourceNodeId":"n0000428","sourceUnitIds":["u000387"],"sourceAnchor":"- Whole-algorithm behavior: add canonicalization and the global schedule, and the paper claims Church–Rosser confluence and optimality for the nonlinear systems. Church–Rosser is the broader agreement property: paths that start together can be continued to a common result—not necessarily in one step.","status":"source_ready"} -->

- Whole-algorithm behavior: add canonicalization and the global schedule, and the paper claims Church–Rosser confluence and optimality for the nonlinear systems. Church–Rosser is the broader agreement property: paths that start together can be continued to a common result—not necessarily in one step.

## 7.3 The broader proposition

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000430","sourceNodeId":"n0000430","sourceUnitIds":["u000389"],"sourceAnchor":"### 7.3 The broader proposition","status":"source_ready"} -->

### 7.3 The broader proposition

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000431","sourceNodeId":"n0000431","sourceUnitIds":["u000390"],"sourceAnchor":"The paper invites you to see λ-calculus as a projection of Δ-Nets. λ-terms force a narrow form of sharing; nets can say sharing relationships that plain syntax hides. If the reduction machinery holds up, that freedom could matter for parallel runtimes and machine architectures.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The paper invites you to see λ-calculus as a projection of Δ-Nets. λ-terms force a narrow form of sharing; nets can say sharing relationships that plain syntax hides. If the reduction machinery holds up, that freedom could matter for parallel runtimes and machine architectures.

✓

## One-sentence takeaway

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000433","sourceNodeId":"n0000433","sourceUnitIds":["u000392"],"sourceAnchor":"### One-sentence takeaway","status":"source_ready"} -->

### One-sentence takeaway

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000435","sourceNodeId":"n0000435","sourceUnitIds":["u000394"],"sourceAnchor":"08 Keep nearby","status":"source_ready"} -->

08 Keep nearby

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000811","sourceNodeId":"n0000811","sourceUnitIds":["u000393"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets encode each sharing context in a variable-arity replicator.","status":"expanded"} -->

A Δ-Net encodes each sharing context using a replicator.

The replicator has variable arity, meaning that its number of sharing connections is not fixed in advance.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000813","sourceNodeId":"n0000813","sourceUnitIds":["u000393"],"sourceAnchor":"The discipline is intended to prevent avoidable work.","status":"source_ready"} -->

The discipline is intended to prevent avoidable work.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-generated-c4dd98e05506d9ed","sourceNodeId":null,"sourceUnitIds":[],"sourceAnchor":"local interaction","status":"expanded"} -->

A local interaction is an interaction between parts that are directly connected or near each other in a structure. Its outcome depends primarily on those parts and their immediate context rather than on the whole system.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000812","sourceNodeId":"n0000812","sourceUnitIds":["u000393"],"sourceAnchor":"Reduction combines local interactions with limited global scheduling discipline.","status":"expanded"} -->

Reduction combines local interactions between nearby structures.

**Limitation.** Reduction also uses a limited amount of global scheduling discipline to coordinate those local interactions.

<!-- refine:source {"id":"teach-n0000434","sourceNodeId":"n0000434","sourceUnitIds":["u000393"],"sourceAnchor":"Δ-Nets tries to make optimal parallel evaluation practical by packing each sharing context into a variable-arity replicator, then combining local interactions with only as much global discipline as you need to avoid preventable work.","status":"source_ready"} -->

Δ-Nets tries to make optimal parallel evaluation practical by packing each sharing context into a variable-arity replicator, then combining local interactions with only as much global discipline as you need to avoid preventable work.
